                                                            GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES AND A HIGHER GENUS
                                                                  GROTHENDIECK-TEICHMÜLLER GROUP


                                                                       LUCIANA BASUALDO BONATTO AND MARCY ROBERTSON


                                                    A BSTRACT. We construct a modular operad 𝐒 of mapping class groupoids modelling the higher-genus
                                                    Teichmüller tower, with compositions and contractions encoding surface gluing. We prove a presenta-
arXiv:2606.01466v2 [math.AT] 8 Oct 2026




                                                    tion theorem for maps out of 𝐒 using genus-zero and genus-one generators and relations. Applying the
                                                    work of Nakamura–Schneps, we obtain a faithful action of their subgroup ̂          ̂ on ̂
                                                                                                                                   ⊆ 𝖦𝖳      𝐒, and hence a
                                                    faithful Galois action. The genus-zero truncation identifies with the cyclic operad of parenthesized rib-
                                                    bon braids, whose object-fixing profinite automorphism group is 𝖦𝖳.̂ The completed classifying spaces
                                                    form a modular ∞-operad whose corolla values are geometric étale homotopy types of moduli stacks
                                                    of curves with nonzero tangent vectors at their marked points. The ̂ -action induces a faithful action by
                                                    homotopy automorphisms on the associated tower of profinite homotopy 1-types, which agrees with the
                                                    étale model in genus zero and one.




                                                                                            1. INTRODUCTION
                                          We construct a modular operad of surface groupoids and prove that it can be reconstructed from its
                                          genus-zero and genus-one parts. This reconstruction gives a presentation for maps into arbitrary mod-
                                          ular operads in groupoids and, after profinite completion, a corresponding determination theorem for
                                          homotopy endomorphisms. We apply these results to the actions of Nakamura–Schneps on profi-
                                          nite mapping class groups, obtaining faithful actions on a modular-operadic model of the Teichmüller
                                          tower and on its associated tower of profinite homotopy 1-types.
                                          In his Esquisse d’un programme, Grothendieck proposed studying Gal(ℚ∕ℚ) through its action on the
                                          moduli spaces of algebraic curves [29]. Let 𝑔,𝑛 denote the moduli stack over ℚ of smooth projective
                                          curves of genus 𝑔 with 𝑛 ordered distinct marked points. Already for 0,4 ≅ ℙ1 ⧵ {0, 1, ∞}, the outer
                                          Galois action on its geometric étale fundamental group is faithful, by Belyi’s theorem [8]. As the
                                          genus and number of marked points vary, the moduli stacks are related by forgetting and relabelling
                                          marked points and by gluing constructions associated with boundary strata of their compactifications.
                                          Grothendieck envisaged organising their geometric étale fundamental groupoids, together with suit-
                                          able compatibility maps, into a Teichmüller tower. This tower offers a way to study the Galois action
                                          through its compatibility with the geometry of moduli spaces in all genera. Our results give an op-
                                          eradic form of his two-level principle, which predicts that the structure of the tower is controlled by
                                          low-genus generators and relations.
                                          For 2𝑔 − 2 + 𝑛 > 0, comparison over ℂ identifies the geometric étale fundamental group of 𝑔,𝑛 with
                                          the profinite completion of the mapping class group of a genus-𝑔 surface with 𝑛 marked points [47, 52].
                                          In the topological approach to the tower developed in [35, 51], one also allows surfaces with boundary,
                                          so that gluing can be described directly in terms of surface topology. Gluing boundary components
                                          and forgetting marked points then induce homomorphisms between mapping class groups, translating
                                          compatibility with the geometric operations in the tower into explicit conditions on automorphisms of
                                          these groups.
                                          To study these Galois actions, it is useful to embed Gal(ℚ∕ℚ) in a group defined by explicit relations
                                          whose action satisfies the same geometric compatibility conditions. In genus zero, this is achieved by
                                          Drinfeld’s profinite Grothendieck–Teichmüller group 𝖦𝖳̂ [18, Section 4]. Drinfeld constructs 𝖦𝖳 ̂ by
                                          studying its action on a tower of profinite braid groups, whose relation to genus-zero mapping class

                                             Date: October 12, 2026.
                                                                                                        1
2                                L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


groups is recalled in Section 3.2. The 𝖦𝖳-action
                                       ̂          on this tower respects forgetting strands and replacing
a strand by several parallel strands (Definition 4.5). Under the embedding
                                                      ̂
                                           Gal(ℚ∕ℚ) ↪ 𝖦𝖳,
it recovers the Galois actions arising from the geometric étale fundamental groups of genus-zero mod-

The strand-replacement operations in this tower of braid groups are encoded by composition in the
operad of parenthesized braids 𝖯𝖺𝖡. In this language, 𝖦𝖳
                                                      ̂ is identified with the group of object-fixing
automorphisms of the profinite completion of 𝖯𝖺𝖡 [6, 24]. This description also has a homotopical
interpretation. The classifying spaces of 𝖯𝖺𝖡 form an operad weakly equivalent to the little 2-disks
operad, and Horel [39] shows that 𝖦𝖳 ̂ is the group of homotopy classes of automorphisms of its
profinite completion.
The framed little 2-disks operad brings this description closer to surface topology. It admits a model
whose spaces are classifying spaces of genus-zero boundary mapping class groups and whose com-
positions are induced by gluing boundary components. Boavida de Brito, Horel, and Robertson [13]
identify 𝖦𝖳
         ̂ with the group of homotopy classes of automorphisms of its profinite completion.
In higher genus, we must also allow two boundary components of the same surface to be glued. This
operation increases the genus and is encoded by the contractions of a modular operad (Definition 2.21).
We construct a modular operad 𝐒 in groupoids whose components satisfy
                               B𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) ≃ BΓ𝑔,𝑛+1           (2𝑔 + 𝑛 ≥ 1),
where Γ𝑔,𝑛+1 is the mapping class group of a genus-𝑔 surface with 𝑛 + 1 boundary components fixed
pointwise (Definition 3.2). Its objects are decorated graphs representing marked pants decomposi-
tions, and its morphisms are mapping classes between the associated surfaces. Its compositions glue
boundary components of separate surfaces, and its contractions glue boundary components of the
same surface. The relation to the genus-zero models is made explicit by the following identification.

Proposition 1.1 (Proposition 3.16). There is an isomorphism of cyclic operads
                                           tr ≤0 𝐒 ≅ 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡cyc .

Here tr ≤0 denotes genus-zero truncation, and 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡cyc is the operad of parenthesized ribbon braids
equipped with the cyclic structure described by Campos, Idrissi, and Willwacher [16, Section 3.1].
This structure treats all boundary components symmetrically, without distinguishing one as an output.
We use the description on generators and the verification of compatibility with the defining relations
and operadic composition given in [55, Section 4.3].
Our central structural result is a presentation for maps from 𝐒 into an arbitrary modular operad in
groupoids.

Theorem 1.2 (Theorem 3.18). Let  be a modular operad in groupoids. A map 𝐒 →  is equivalent to
the choice of an object 𝐦 ∈ ob((0, 3)) and four morphisms 𝐭, 𝐛, 𝐚, 𝐬, representing a twist, a braiding,
an associator, and a genus-one switch, subject to the conditions specified in Theorem 3.18. These
conditions involve only genus zero and one, with three additional relations in genus one.

The presentation gives a reconstruction theorem for the modular operad itself. Since all generators
and relations occur in genus at most one, every map from tr ≤1 𝐒 to tr ≤1  extends uniquely to a map
𝐒 → . Equivalently, the counit of the genus-one truncation adjunction is an isomorphism
                                           (tr ≤1 )! (tr ≤1 𝐒) ≅ 𝐒,
where (tr ≤1 )! is left adjoint to genus-one truncation. This is a universal property describing maps into
every modular operad in groupoids, and gives an operadic form of the Lego–Teichmüller constructions
of [4].
                                      GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                        3


The geometric input to the presentation is the two-dimensional CW complex (𝑆) of markings over
pants decompositions of a parametrised, labelled surface 𝑆 (Definition 2.37). Building on [36, 35, 4],
this complex has marked pants decompositions as vertices, elementary moves as edges, and relations
among these moves as 2-cells. Twists and braidings change the markings on individual pairs of pants,
while associativity and genus-one moves change the underlying pants decomposition. Starting from
the complex of maximal markings of [4], we retain only decompositions into pairs of pants and adapt
the 2-cell relations to this setting. These include explicit gluing relations for Dehn twists and a modi-
fied relation on a genus-one surface with two boundary components.

Proposition 1.3 (Proposition 2.38). For a parametrised, labelled surface 𝑆, the complex (𝑆) of
markings over pants decompositions is connected and simply connected.

We prove this in Appendix C by comparing the marking complex with the pants-decomposition com-
plex, controlling the additional marking data and the relations introduced by gluing boundary com-
ponents. Lemma 3.7 then relates paths in (𝑆) to morphisms in 𝐒. Connectedness supplies the
generators, while simple connectedness ensures that the prescribed relations suffice. Compatibility
with gluing turns this description into a presentation of the modular operad itself.
Passing to profinite completion gives a modular operad ̂𝐒 in profinite groupoids (Section 4.1). The
reconstruction theorem and the profinite-completion adjunction give an isomorphism of monoids
                                       End0 (̂
                                             𝐒) ≅ End0 (tr ≤1 ̂
                                                              𝐒),
where the subscript 0 denotes endomorphisms fixing the objects of the underlying groupoids. Thus ev-
ery object-fixing endomorphism of the truncated modular operad extends uniquely to the full profinite
surface operad.
We apply this determination result to the arithmetic actions constructed by Nakamura and Schneps.
They introduced a subgroup ̂      ̂ containing the image of Gal(ℚ∕ℚ), and constructed actions of
                              ⊆ 𝖦𝖳
 on profinite mapping class groups using quilt decompositions of surfaces. They describe changes
̂
of quilt decomposition by explicit inner automorphisms and prove compatibility with inclusions of
subsurfaces carrying the induced decomposition [51, Theorems 1.3–1.4].
Our markings determine the curves and Dehn twists appearing in their formulae. The presentation
theorem reduces the construction of an action on ̂   𝐒 to specifying the images of the generating mor-
phisms and checking the defining relations in genus zero and one. It then supplies a unique extension
preserving compositions, contractions, and symmetric-group actions. The resulting ̂   -action is faith-
ful, and its genus-zero restriction agrees with the 𝖦𝖳-action
                                                    ̂         along ̂
                                                                     ↪ 𝖦𝖳̂ (Theorem 4.9). Restricting
this action gives the following result.

Theorem 1.4 (Consequence of Theorem 4.9). The absolute Galois group Gal(ℚ∕ℚ) acts faithfully
on ̂
   𝐒 through an explicit homomorphism
                                    Gal(ℚ∕ℚ) ↪ Aut 0 (̂

where Aut 0 denotes automorphisms fixing the objects of the underlying groupoids.

The modular dendroidal nerve N lets us regard a strict modular operad as a modular ∞-operad, whose
compositions and contractions are compatible up to coherent homotopy. We work with a genus-graded
version of the graphical Segal model introduced in [32, 33], recalled in Section 5. We prove that the
nerve is homotopically fully faithful (Theorem B.2), so this passage preserves derived mapping spaces
and allows us to study homotopy endomorphisms in either model.
For ̂
    𝐒, we combine this comparison with the explicit presentation to identify homotopy classes of
automorphisms:
                                       Aut 0 (̂
                                              𝐒) ≅ 𝜋0 ℝ Aut(N̂𝐒)
(Proposition 6.12). Every homotopy-automorphism class therefore has a unique object-fixing strict
representative, and the arithmetic action remains faithful after passage to homotopy classes.
4                                L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


The final part of the paper relates these results to the étale homotopy types of moduli stacks of curves.
We use the formulation of étale homotopy types as shapes of étale ∞-topoi [46, 17], compatible with
the classical constructions of [3, 25]. In the stable range, Oda’s comparison identifies the profinite
étale homotopy types of moduli stacks of smooth pointed curves with the profinite completions of
the classifying spaces of their mapping class groups [52, Theorem 1]. For boundary mapping class
groups, the corresponding moduli problem includes a nonzero tangent vector at each marked point.
Writing tan𝑔,𝑛+1
                  for this moduli stack over ℚ, we establish an equivalence
                                ́ ̂
                               Π𝑒𝑡  tan        ̂             (2𝑔 + 𝑛 ≥ 1)
                                ∞ (𝑔,𝑛+1 ) ≃ BΓ𝑔,𝑛+1
in Lemma 6.17.
These profinite spaces are therefore the corolla values of BN𝐒.

preserve products in general, verifying the modular Segal condition requires a separate product com-
parison. We establish this in Proposition 6.18 using finite CW models for boundary mapping class
groups and the criterion of [34]. It follows that BN𝐒
                                                  ̂ is a modular ∞-operad whose corolla values are
profinite étale homotopy types and whose structure maps encode gluing (Lemma 6.19).
Passing to profinite homotopy 1-types gives a natural comparison

On corollas, this replaces the profinite étale homotopy types by their homotopy 1-types, giving a
modular ∞-operad modelling the tower of profinite fundamental groupoids. The strict-to-homotopy
comparison and the homotopical full faithfulness of the profinite classifying-space functor give the
following action on this tower.

Goodness of the boundary mapping class groups in genus zero and one implies that the comparison
is an equivalence after genus-one truncation (Proposition 6.22). Combining this equivalence with the
reconstruction theorem and the comparison of strict and homotopy endomorphisms gives a two-level
theorem for the entire homotopy-endomorphism monoid.

Thus restriction determines all homotopy classes of endomorphisms of the full operadic tower of
profinite homotopy 1-types. Every endomorphism of the genus-zero and genus-one truncated modular
∞-operad of étale homotopy types extends uniquely at the level of homotopy classes to this tower. The
determination of the Nakamura–Schneps and Galois actions is a consequence of this general statement.
1.1. Organization of the Paper. Following the preliminaries, we construct and present the surface
operad 𝐒, prove its reconstruction from genus zero and one, and construct the profinite arithmetic
actions. Sections 5 and 6 then develop the homotopical setting, the comparison of endomorphisms,
and the relation to étale homotopy types.
The homotopical results used in the paper are developed in Appendices A and B. The first treats
model structures on modular operads and their genus truncations. The second proves homotopical
full faithfulness of the modular dendroidal nerve, allowing derived mapping spaces to be computed in
                                       GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                        5


either the strict or the graphical Segal model. These results underlie the comparison of automorphisms
and the homotopical two-level principle in Section 6. Appendix C supplies the geometric input to the
presentation theorem by proving connectedness and simple connectedness of the marking complexes.

                                                  2. P RELIMINARIES
Throughout, we write ℰ = (ℰ , ⊗, 1ℰ ) for a cocomplete, cartesian symmetric monoidal category
whose monoidal product ⊗ commutes with small colimits in each variable. In this paper, ℰ will be
(completed versions of) the category of sets, groupoids, or simplicial sets. We work with categories
enriched, tensored, and cotensored over simplicial sets. Thus, for objects 𝑋, 𝑌 ∈ ℰ , there is a sim-
plicial mapping space1 Mapℰ (𝑋, 𝑌 ), whose set of 0-simplices is Homℰ (𝑋, 𝑌 ). To reduce clutter, we
often suppress the category ℰ and write Hom(𝑋, 𝑌 ) or Map(𝑋, 𝑌 ) when no confusion can arise.

In order to discuss homotopy classes of maps between (modular and cyclic) operads, we will make
use of the language of Quillen model categories and ∞-categories. For Quillen model categories, we
take [38] or [5] as comprehensive references. We will use the formalism of relative categories from
[7] or [5, Chapter 13] as our model for ∞-categories.

Definition 2.1. A relative category is a pair (ℰ , 𝒲 ) where ℰ is a small category and 𝒲 is a wide
subcategory of ℰ whose morphisms are a class of designated weak equivalences in ℰ . A functor of
relative categories 𝐹 ∶ (ℰ , 𝒲 ) → (ℰ ′ , 𝒲 ′ ) consists of a functor 𝐹 ∶ ℰ → ℰ ′ such that 𝐹 (𝒲 ) ⊂ 𝒲 ′ .

Example 2.2. Any model category ℰ is a relative category in which 𝒲 is the class of weak equiva-
lences in the model structure on ℰ .

The homotopy category of a relative category ℰ , denoted Ho(ℰ ), is obtained by formally inverting
the morphisms in 𝒲 . To describe maps in the homotopy category Ho(ℰ ) we use the Dwyer-Kan
enhancement of the homotopy category. This is a simplicially enriched category, (ℰ , 𝒲 ), which has
the same objects as ℰ and, for any two objects 𝑋, 𝑌 ∈ ob(ℰ ), a space of maps ℝ Map(𝑋, 𝑌 ).2 By
considering ℰ to be a discrete simplicial category, one can define a natural inclusion ℰ → (ℰ , 𝒲 ),
with the property that, for every 𝑋, 𝑌 ∈ ob(ℰ ),
                                      HomHo(ℰ ) (𝑋, 𝑌 ) = 𝜋0 ℝ Mapℰ (𝑋, 𝑌 ).
The fact that the connected components of ℝ Mapℰ (𝑋, 𝑌 ) correspond to morphisms in ℰ reflects that
the simplicial category (ℰ , 𝒲 ) captures the higher homotopical data of morphisms in the homotopy
category of ℰ . In particular, for any weak equivalence 𝑋 → 𝑋 ′ and any 𝑌 ∈ ob(ℰ ) in ℰ the induced
maps
        ℝ Mapℰ (𝑋 ′ , 𝑌 )           ℝ Mapℰ (𝑋, 𝑌 )          and      ℝ Mapℰ (𝑌 , 𝑋)               ℝ Mapℰ (𝑌 , 𝑋 ′ )
are weak equivalences of spaces.
In general, it is difficult to compute the derived mapping spaces ℝ Mapℰ (𝑋, 𝑌 ). However, if ℰ admits
the structure of a simplicial model category ([38, Chapter 9]), we have the following useful fact ([22,
Corollary 4.7]):

Theorem 2.3. Let ℰ be a simplicial model category. If 𝑋 is cofibrant and 𝑌 is fibrant in ℰ , then
there exists a natural zigzag of weak equivalences
                                          Mapℰ (𝑋, 𝑌 ) ≃ ℝ Mapℰ (𝑋, 𝑌 ).

Let 𝒞 and 𝒟 be two simplicial model categories. A simplicial Quillen adjunction between two sim-
                                      F
plicial model categories 𝒞                  𝒟 gives rise to a homotopy adjunction
                                      G

                                                            𝕃F
(2.1)                                      (𝒞 , 𝒲𝒞 )             (𝒟 , 𝒲𝒟 ).
                                                            ℝG

    1We follow a common abuse of terminology and often use the term space when referring to simplicial sets.
   2Here, the ℝ is to indicate that we are taking the “right derived” version of the Map(−, −) functor. This is also commonly
denoted Mapℎ (𝑋, 𝑌 ) in the literature, e.g. [38].

This means that there exists a weak equivalence of spaces
                             ℝ Map𝒟 (𝕃F(𝑋), 𝑌 ) ≃ ℝ Map𝒞 (𝑋, ℝG(𝑌 )),
for all 𝑋 ∈ 𝒞 and 𝑌 ∈ 𝒟 . Here, the notation 𝕃 and ℝ denotes that we are using the left and right
derived functors of F and G, respectively.
2.1. Adjunctions between spaces and groupoids. Let 𝒮 denote the category of simplicial sets with
its usual Kan-Quillen simplicial model structure (e.g. [5, Chapter 6.1]) and let 𝒢 denote the category
of groupoids equipped with the model structure in which weak equivalences are equivalences of cat-
egories, cofibrations are morphisms that are injective on objects, and fibrations are isofibrations (e.g.
[2], [15]). Note that in this model structure, every object in 𝒢 is both fibrant and cofibrant.
The functor which sends a space 𝑋 to its fundamental groupoid Π1 (𝑋), is the left adjoint in an ad-
junction
(2.2)                                    Π1 ∶ 𝒮             𝒢 ∶ B.
The right adjoint is called the classifying space functor. The category of groupoids becomes a simpli-
cial category via this adjunction. In particular, one defines the space of maps between two groupoids
as
                                   Map𝒢 (𝒞 , 𝒟 ) ∶= Map𝒮 (B𝒞 , B𝒟 ).
The classifying space functor preserves and reflects weak equivalences and fibrations which makes
(2.2) into a simplicial Quillen adjunction in which the right adjoint is homotopically fully faithful in
the sense that the induced map
                              ℝ Map𝒢 (𝒞 , 𝒟 )           ℝ Map𝒮 (B𝒞 , B𝒟 )
is a weak equivalence of spaces for all 𝒞 , 𝒟 ∈ 𝒢 .
2.2. Profinite completion of spaces and groupoids. For any small category ℰ , the category of pro-
objects in ℰ , denoted Pro(ℰ ), can be thought of as the category obtained by freely adding all co-
filtered limits to ℰ . More precisely, one can describe the category of pro-objects in ℰ , Pro(ℰ ), as the
category whose objects are pairs (𝐼, 𝑋) where 𝐼 is a cofiltered category and 𝑋 = {𝑋𝑖 }𝑖∈𝐼 is a diagram
𝑋 ∶ 𝐼 → ℰ . If 𝑋 ∶ 𝐼 → ℰ and 𝑌 ∶ 𝐽 → ℰ are cofiltered diagrams, then morphisms in Pro(ℰ ) are
defined by
                          HomPro(ℰ ) ({𝑋}𝑖 , {𝑌 }𝑗 ) = lim colim Homℰ (𝑋𝑖 , 𝑌𝑗 ).
                                                      𝑗∈𝐽    𝑖∈𝐼
Similarly, given a full subcategory 𝒞 ⊆ ℰ , we write Proℰ (𝒞 ) ⊆ Pro(ℰ ) for the full subcategory
spanned by those pro-objects (𝐼, 𝑋) such that 𝑋𝑖 ∈ 𝒞 for all 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼; equivalently, Proℰ (𝒞 ) ≅ Pro(𝒞 ).
If the category ℰ is complete and 𝒞 ⊆ ℰ is a full subcategory closed under finite limits, we can define
an adjunction

(2.3)                               ̂ ∶ℰ
                                   (−)                Proℰ (𝒞 ) ∶ | − |.

The left adjoint, (−),
                   ̂ is called the pro-𝒞 -completion functor. The right adjoint, |−|, sends a pro-object
{𝑋𝑖 }𝑖∈𝐼 to its limit in ℰ . If, moreover, ℰ is a cofibrantly generated simplicial model category and
𝒞 ⊆ ℰ satisfies a specific list of conditions (e.g., the conditions of a fibration test category in [12,
Definition 5.1]) then Proℰ (𝒞 ) admits a fibrantly generated model category structure and the adjunction
(2.3) becomes a simplicial Quillen pair.
2.2.1. Profinite groupoids. Let 𝖦𝗋𝗉 denote the category of discrete groups and let 𝖦𝗋𝗉f in ⊆ 𝖦𝗋𝗉 be
the full subcategory of finite groups. The pro-category
                                         ̂ ∶= Pro𝖦𝗋𝗉 (𝖦𝗋𝗉f in )
                                         𝖦𝗋𝗉
is the category of profinite groups. There is an adjunction
                                      ̂ ∶ 𝖦𝗋𝗉
                                     (−)                  ̂ ∶|−|
                                                          𝖦𝗋𝗉
8                                L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


in which the right adjoint sends a pro-object 𝑋 = {𝑋𝑗 }𝑗∈𝐽 to its limit in 𝖦𝗋𝗉. The left adjoint is the
classical profinite completion functor which sends a group 𝖦 to the pro-object {𝖦∕𝖭}𝖭⊲𝑓 𝖦 indexed
by the cofiltered poset of finite-index normal subgroups 𝖭 ⊲𝑓 𝖦. Equivalently, one may identify 𝖦     ̂
with the inverse limit

                                           𝖦

in 𝖦𝗋𝗉.

Example 2.4. Let 𝑋 be a connected, locally Noetherian scheme and let 𝑥̄ → 𝑋 be a geometric point.

Example 2.5. Let 𝑛𝑔 denote the moduli stack of smooth genus 𝑔 curves with 𝑛 marked points, defined
over ℚ. For 2𝑔 − 2 + 𝑛 > 0, the geometric 𝑒𝑡𝑎𝑙𝑒́  fundamental group of 𝑛𝑔 identifies, after choosing
a geometric base point, with the profinite completion of the mapping class group of a genus 𝑔 surface
with 𝑛 marked points fixed pointwise:
                                             (         )
                                        𝜋1𝑒𝑡́ 𝑛𝑔 ⊗ℚ ℚ ≅ Γ ̂𝑛 .
                                                             𝑔
See [47, 14] for more details.

The profinite completion functor for groups admits a direct analogue for groupoids. We say that a
groupoid ℰ is finite if it has finitely many morphisms (hence finitely many objects), and write 𝒢f in ⊆ 𝒢
for the full subcategory of finite groupoids. Since 𝒢f in is closed under finite limits, we define the
category of profinite groupoids by
                                             𝒢̂ ∶= Pro𝒢 (𝒢f in ).

The category of profinite groupoids 𝒢̂ admits a cocombinatorial model structure, and the profinite
completion functor fits into a Quillen adjunction

                                        ̂ ∶𝒢
                                       (−)                   𝒢̂ ∶ | − |

(see [39, Theorem 4.12 and Proposition 4.22]), where | − | denotes the limit functor 𝒢̂ → 𝒢 . The fol-
lowing proposition shows that, for groupoids with a finite set of objects, profinite completion preserves
binary products.

Proposition 2.6. [39, Proposition 4.23] Let ℰ and 𝒟 be groupoids with a finite set of objects. Then
the canonical map
                                        ℰ̂× 𝒟 ⟶ ℰ̂ × 𝒟̂
is an isomorphism in 𝒢̂.

2.2.2. Profinite spaces. A simplicial set is called lean if it is degreewise finite and 𝑛-coskeletal for
some 𝑛 ≥ 0. Let 𝒮lean ⊆ 𝒮 denote the full subcategory of lean simplicial sets. This subcategory is
closed under finite limits and cotensors by finite simplicial sets. We define the category of profinite
spaces by
                                          𝒮̂ ∶= Pro𝒮 (𝒮lean ).
It is equivalent to the category of simplicial profinite sets [12, Example 5.5].
We equip 𝒮̂ with Quick’s model structure [53, Theorem 2.12], in which cofibrations are monomor-
phisms and weak equivalences are detected by 𝜋0 , 𝜋1 , and cohomology with finite local coefficients.
                                               GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                           9


This is also the model structure obtained from [12, Theorem 5.2]; see [12, Example 5.5 and Corol-
lary 6.6]. Profinite completion and the limit functor form a Quillen adjunction [53, Proposition 2.28]:
                                                 ̂ ∶𝒮
                                                (−)                         𝒮̂ ∶ | − |.

The classifying-space functor sends finite groupoids to lean simplicial sets and induces a simplicial
Quillen adjunction [12, Example 7.7]:
                                                  ̂ 1 ∶ 𝒮̂
                                                  Π                             𝒢̂ ∶ B.
                                                                                     ̂

Moreover, these functors fit into a commutative square of simplicial Quillen adjunctions [12, Exam-
ple 7.14]:
                                                                     Π1
                                                           𝒢                    𝒮
                                                                     B
                                                      ̂
                                                     (−)       |−|        |−|         ̂
                                                                                     (−)
                                                                     ̂1
                                                                     Π
                                                           𝒢̂                   𝒮̂
                                                                     ̂
                                                                     B

Example 2.7. Artin–Mazur [3] and Friedlander [25] construct the étale homotopy type of a scheme
as a pro-simplicial set. We write Π𝑒𝑡́ (𝑋) for this pro-simplicial set, and write Π
                                                                                  ̂ 𝑒𝑡́ (𝑋) for its profi-
nite completion, viewed as a profinite space. The same notation will be used for stacks, using étale
hypercovers. In Section 6.4, we use the corresponding ∞-categorical formulation, where the étale
homotopy type is viewed as a pro-object in the ∞-category of spaces.
Let 𝑛𝑔 denote the moduli stack of smooth genus-𝑔 curves with 𝑛 distinct ordered marked points,
defined over ℚ. Oda considers the étale homotopy type of the geometric stack 𝑛𝑔 ⊗ℚ ℚ. If Γ𝑛𝑔
denotes the mapping class group of a genus-𝑔 surface with 𝑛 marked points fixed pointwise, then, for
2𝑔 − 2 + 𝑛 > 0, [52, Theorem 1] gives a weak equivalence of profinite spaces
                                           (        )
                                     Π̂ 𝑒𝑡́ 𝑛 ⊗ℚ ℚ ≃ BΓ ̂𝑛 .
                                             𝑔              𝑔
Equivalently, the profinite étale homotopy type is the profinite completion of the classifying space of
the mapping class group Γ𝑛𝑔 .

2.3. Graphs. We model the operations of operads, cyclic operads, modular operads and their ∞-
versions with decorated graphs.

                                            𝑛 = Aut({0, 1, … , 𝑛}); then Σ𝑛 identifies naturally with
Notation 2.8. Let Σ𝑛 = Aut({1, … , 𝑛}) and Σ+
the subgroup of Σ+
                 𝑛 consisting of permutations that fix 0.

Definition 2.9. A graph 𝐺 consists of a (possibly empty) finite set of vertices 𝑉𝐺 , a finite set of half-
edges 𝐴𝐺 ,3 together with gluing data encoded by a diagram of finite sets
                                                                                       𝑡
                                           †       𝐴𝐺                    𝐷𝐺                𝑉𝐺 .
Here † ∶ 𝐴𝐺 → 𝐴𝐺 is a fixed-point-free involution, and 𝐷𝐺 ⊆ 𝐴𝐺 is the subset of half-edges adjacent
to vertices, with 𝑡 ∶ 𝐷𝐺 → 𝑉𝐺 recording the incident vertex.

An edge of 𝐺 is an orbit of †, written 𝑒 = [𝑎, 𝑎† ], and we write 𝐸𝐺 = {[𝑎, 𝑎† ] ∣ 𝑎 ∈ 𝐴𝐺 } for the set
of edges. For 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉𝐺 , the neighborhood of 𝑣 is the set of incident half-edges 𝑛𝑏(𝑣) ∶= 𝑡−1 (𝑣) ⊆ 𝐷𝐺 ;
its elements are said to be adjacent to 𝑣. An edge 𝑒 = [𝑎, 𝑎† ] is internal if 𝑎, 𝑎† ∈ 𝐷𝐺 ; we write
𝑖𝐸𝐺 ⊆ 𝐸𝐺 for the set of internal edges. The remaining half-edges
                                                     𝜕(𝐺) ∶= 𝐴𝐺 ⧵ 𝐷𝐺
are called legs, and 𝜕(𝐺) is called the boundary of 𝐺.
   3Half-edges are also called “arcs” in the literature, hence the use of the letter 𝐴 for the set of half-edges.
10                               L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


Example 2.10.       (1) The graph consisting of a single edge and no vertices is denoted ↕. Con-
      cretely, it has 𝑉↕ = ∅, 𝐷↕ = ∅, and 𝐴↕ = {𝑎, 𝑎† } with †(𝑎) = 𝑎† .
     (2) The (𝑛 + 1)st corolla, denoted 𝐶𝑛+1 , is the graph with a single vertex 𝑣 and no internal edges.
         It has 𝑛 + 1 half-edges adjacent to 𝑣, and each of these is paired under † with a boundary
         half-edge. These boundary half-edges are the 𝑛 + 1 legs of the corolla.
Many additional examples of graphs can be found in [32], [33], and in the survey paper [31].

A graph 𝐻 is a subgraph of a graph 𝐺 if there is a morphism of the defining diagrams
                                                               𝑡
                                  †      𝐴𝐻           𝐷𝐻                𝑉𝐻

                                                               𝑡
                                   †     𝐴𝐺           𝐷𝐺                𝑉𝐺
such that the square
                                            𝐷𝐻           𝐷𝐺
                                              𝑡            𝑡

                                            𝑉𝐻           𝑉𝐺
is a pullback and the induced map 𝑉𝐻 → 𝑉𝐺 is injective (cf. [32, Definition 1.13]).

Example 2.11. If 𝐺 has at least one vertex and 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉𝐺 , there is a canonical subgraph 𝐶𝑣 ↪ 𝐺, called
the corolla at 𝑣 (cf. [32, Definition 1.5]). It has a single vertex {𝑣}, and its adjacent half-edges are
exactly the elements of 𝑛𝑏(𝑣). Each such half-edge is paired with a boundary half-edge in 𝐶𝑣 , so the
legs of 𝐶𝑣 are naturally in bijection with 𝑛𝑏(𝑣).

For graphs with at least one vertex, the gluing data of 𝐺 can be assembled into a coequalizer diagram
(cf. [32, Construction 1.18]).

Lemma 2.12. Let Fin denote the category of finite sets, and let ℐ be the category with three objects
and three generating morphisms of the shape
                                            ∙        ∙             ∙.
A connected graph 𝐺 with at least one vertex, viewed as an object of Finℐ , is the coequalizer
                              ∐                  ∐
                                𝑒∈𝑖𝐸𝐺 ↕            𝑣∈𝑉𝐺 𝐶𝑣         𝐺.

The two arrows send each internal edge to the two corresponding boundary legs of the corollas at its
incident vertices.

2.3.1. Additional structure on graphs. Our combinatorial model admits a geometric realization |𝐺|
obtained by gluing a point for each vertex and a closed interval for each edge, identifying the endpoints
of each interval with the incident vertices via the incidence map 𝑡 ∶ 𝐷𝐺 → 𝑉𝐺 . We say that 𝐺 is
connected (respectively, simply connected) if |𝐺| is connected (respectively, simply connected) (see
also [32, Definition 1.7]).

Definition 2.13. A tree is a simply connected graph. A tree 𝑇 is rooted if it is equipped with a
distinguished leg 𝑟 ∈ 𝜕(𝑇 ), called the root.

Remark 2.14. Any rooted tree admits a canonical orientation by directing every edge toward the root.
Since 𝑇 is simply connected, each vertex then has a unique outgoing incident edge (the one on the
path to the root), and all remaining incident edges are incoming (cf. [31, Definition 3.4]).

In order to use graphs to model operations in modular and cyclic operads we will equip them with
additional labeling data.
                                         GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                  11


Definition 2.15. A graph 𝐺 is said to be labelled if:
                                                                                               ≅
    (1) it comes with a total ordering of its vertices, i.e. a bijection 𝜆 ∶ {1, … , 𝑘} ←←←→
                                                                                           ← 𝑉𝐺 ;
    (2) for each vertex 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉𝐺 , it comes with a bijection
                                                                      ≅
                                    𝓁𝑣 ∶ {0, 1, … , |𝑛𝑏(𝑣)| − 1} ←←←→
                                                                    ← 𝑛𝑏(𝑣);

    (3) it comes with a bijection of the boundary (legs)
                                                ≅
                        𝓁𝐺 ∶ {0, 1, … , 𝑛} ←←←→
                                              ← 𝜕(𝐺),          where 𝑛 = |𝜕(𝐺)| − 1.

We write (𝐺, 𝜆, 𝓁) for this data.

A genus grading on a graph 𝐺 is a function 𝜖 ∶ 𝑉𝐺 → ℕ. The genus of a genus-graded graph (𝐺, 𝜖) is
                                                  ∑
                                         𝛽1 (𝐺) +   𝜖(𝑣),
                                                        𝑣∈𝑉𝐺

where 𝛽1 (𝐺) is the first Betti number of the geometric realization of 𝐺. If 𝐺 is connected and 𝐺 ≠↕,
then
                                      𝛽1 (𝐺) = |𝑖𝐸𝐺 | − |𝑉𝐺 | + 1.
We adopt the convention that the edge ↕ has genus zero.

Notation 2.16. Graphs in this paper are often equipped with additional structure, such as a vertex-
ordering 𝜆, labelings 𝓁 (Definition 2.15), and/or a genus grading 𝜖. When we wish to display this data
explicitly we write
                                            𝐺 = (𝐺, 𝜆, 𝓁, 𝜖),
omitting any entries that are not present. When no confusion can arise, we suppress some or all of
these decorations and simply write 𝐺 for the underlying graph.

Definition 2.17. An isomorphism of graphs 𝜑 ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐺′ is a triple of bijections
                               ≅                          ≅                            ≅
                   𝜑𝐴 ∶ 𝐴𝐺 ←←←→
                              ← 𝐴𝐺′ ,           𝜑𝐷 ∶ 𝐷𝐺 ←←←→
                                                           ← 𝐷𝐺′ ,          𝜑𝑉 ∶ 𝑉𝐺 ←←←→
                                                                                       ← 𝑉𝐺′
such that the following diagram commutes:
                                                                    𝑡𝐺
                                    †𝐺      𝐴𝐺            𝐷𝐺               𝑉𝐺
                                           𝜑𝐴                  𝜑𝐷             𝜑𝑉

                                   †𝐺′      𝐴𝐺′           𝐷𝐺′       𝑡𝐺 ′
                                                                           𝑉𝐺 ′ .

If 𝐺 and 𝐺′ are labelled graphs, a strict isomorphism 𝜑 ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐺′ is an isomorphism that preserves
the vertex ordering and the labelings.

2.4. Operads and cyclic operads. A symmetric sequence in ℰ is a collection of objects  = {(𝑛)}𝑛≥0
in ℰ , together with a right action of the symmetric group Σ𝑛 on each (𝑛). In particular, for each 𝑛 ≥ 0
and each 𝜎 ∈ Σ𝑛 , there is a morphism
                                             𝜎 ∗ ∶ (𝑛) → (𝑛).

Definition 2.18. An operad in ℰ consists of a symmetric sequence  = {(𝑛)}𝑛≥1 together with:
      ∙ a distinguished element id ∈ (1);
      ∙ partial compositions
                                                ◦𝑖
                          (𝑛) × (𝑚)                (𝑛 + 𝑚 − 1),            1 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑛,

satisfying the following axioms, for all 𝑥 ∈ (𝑛), 𝑦 ∈ (𝑚), 𝑧 ∈ (𝓁):
12                                L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


     (1) For 1 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑛 and 1 ≤ 𝑗 ≤ 𝑚,
                                        (𝑥◦𝑖 𝑦)◦𝑖+𝑗−1 𝑧 = 𝑥◦𝑖 (𝑦◦𝑗 𝑧).

     (2) For 1 ≤ 𝑖 < 𝑘 ≤ 𝑛,
                                       (𝑥◦𝑖 𝑦)◦𝑘+𝑚−1 𝑧 = (𝑥◦𝑘 𝑧)◦𝑖 𝑦.
     (3) For 𝑥 ∈ (𝑛) and 1 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑛, id ◦1 𝑥 = 𝑥, and 𝑥◦𝑖 id = 𝑥.
     (4) For 𝜎 ∈ Σ𝑛 and 𝜏 ∈ Σ𝑚 ,
                                    (𝜎 ∗ 𝑥)◦𝑖 (𝜏 ∗ 𝑦) = (𝜎◦𝑖 𝜏)∗ (𝑥◦𝜎 −1 (𝑖) 𝑦),
         where 𝜎◦𝑖 𝜏 ∈ Σ𝑛+𝑚−1 denotes the standard block permutation determined by inserting 𝜏 into
         the 𝑖th input of 𝜎.

A map 𝑓 ∶  →  of operads is a Σ𝑛 -equivariant map of symmetric sequences that preserves the unit
and partial compositions. We write Op(ℰ ) for the category of operads in ℰ .

A cyclic operad is an operad in which the symmetric group actions on inputs extend compatibly to
actions on the output together with the inputs. We write
                                          Σ+
                                           𝑛 ∶= Aut({0, 1, … , 𝑛})

         𝑛 ∈ Σ𝑛 denote the cyclic permutation 𝑧𝑛 (𝑖) = 𝑖 + 1 (mod 𝑛 + 1).
and let 𝑧+    +                                +

                                          {

A cyclic operad is an operad equipped with a cyclic structure.

A map 𝑓 ∶  →  of cyclic operads is a map of operads commuting with the Σ+    𝑛 -actions. We write
Cyc(ℰ ) for the category of cyclic operads in ℰ . As discussed in Appendix A, the categories Op(ℰ )
and Cyc(ℰ ) admit model category structures in which a map 𝑓 ∶  →  is a weak equivalence if and
only if each 𝑓 (𝑛) ∶ (𝑛) → (𝑛) is a weak equivalence in ℰ for all 𝑛 ≥ 0.

Remark 2.20. Cyclic operads are often presented using two-slot partial compositions
                                  ◦𝑗𝑖 ∶ (𝑛) × (𝑚) ⟶ (𝑛 + 𝑚 − 1),
where 𝑖 ∈ {0, … , 𝑛} and 𝑗 ∈ {0, … , 𝑚}, rather than using the one-slot compositions ◦𝑖 of an ordinary
operad together with an extended symmetric group action. The two formalisms are equivalent.
Indeed, using the Σ+𝑚 -action on (𝑚), one may first relabel the second operation so that its 𝑗th slot
becomes the distinguished slot 0. One then composes using the operation ◦0𝑖 , and finally reindexes the
output using the induced Σ+ 𝑛+𝑚−1
                                  -action. For example, if 𝑧+
                                                            𝑚 ∈ Σ𝑚 denotes the cycle 𝑧𝑚 (𝑟) = 𝑟 + 1
                                                                   +                      +

(mod 𝑚 + 1), then, up to the chosen convention for the final labelling, one may write
                                               ( (              ))
                                   𝑥◦𝑗𝑖 𝑦 ∶= 𝜌∗ 𝑥◦0𝑖 ((𝑧+ −𝑗 ∗
                                                        𝑚) ) 𝑦 ,
                                       GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                      13


where 𝜌 ∈ Σ+ 𝑛+𝑚−1
                    is the permutation determined by the chosen labelling convention for the boundary
of the output. For an explicit computation of 𝜌, see [19].

2.5. Modular operads. For a fixed monoidal category ℰ , a genus-graded sequence in ℰ is a collec-
tion of objects
                                     = {(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)}𝑔≥0,𝑛≥0
in ℰ , such that each (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) carries a right action of the extended symmetric group Σ+𝑛 ∶=
Aut({0, 1, … , 𝑛}).

Definition 2.21. A modular operad in ℰ consists of a genus-graded sequence  = {(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)}
equipped with the following structure.
    (1) A distinguished unit object id ∈ (0, 2).
    (2) A family of partial compositions
                                                                    𝑗
                                                                  ◦𝑖
                         (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) × (ℎ, 𝑚 + 1)                      (𝑔 + ℎ, 𝑛 + 𝑚),
        for 𝑛 + 𝑚 ≥ 1, 0 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑛, and 0 ≤ 𝑗 ≤ 𝑚.
    (3) A family of contractions
                                                     𝑗
                                                   𝜉𝑖
                                 (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)                 (𝑔 + 1, 𝑛 − 1),
        for 𝑛 ≥ 2 and 0 ≤ 𝑖 < 𝑗 ≤ 𝑛.
The composition maps satisfy the associativity, equivariance, and unitality axioms of [37, Defini-
tion 3.3.1] or [28, Theorem 3.7]. The contractions satisfy the following compatibility axioms.

A map 𝑓 ∶  →  of modular operads is a map of the underlying genus-graded sequences that is
equivariant for the Σ+
                     𝑛 -actions and commutes with the unit, partial compositions, and contractions.
We write Mod(ℰ ) for the category of modular operads in ℰ . As we discuss in Theorem A.5, Mod(ℰ )
admits a model category structure in which a map 𝑓 ∶  →  is a weak equivalence if each component
                              𝑓 (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) ∶ (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) ⟶ (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)
is a weak equivalence in ℰ for all 𝑔 ≥ 0 and 𝑛 ≥ 0.
14                                L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


Remark 2.22. Our indexing conventions for modular operads are chosen to match the mapping class
group conventions of Section 2.6. Thus we write a genus-graded sequence as {(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)}𝑔≥0,𝑛≥0 , so
that the boundary labels are {0, 1, … , 𝑛} and the symmetric group action is by Σ+
                                                                                 𝑛 = Aut({0, 1, … , 𝑛}).
This is a slight shift from the convention used in parts of the literature, where one often writes
{(𝑔, 𝑛)}𝑔,𝑛≥0 .
We also exclude operations with no boundary components. Equivalently, in the above structure maps
we only include compositions and contractions whose targets still have nonempty boundary; see Ap-
pendix A.

2.5.1. Truncations of modular operads and adjunctions. In Appendix A.1 we define the genus-𝑔 trun-
cation of a modular operad
                                     = {(ℎ, 𝑛 + 1)}ℎ≥0,𝑛≥0 ,
denoted tr ≤𝑔 , as the truncated modular operad obtained by retaining only the operations whose
output has genus at most 𝑔. Thus compositions and contractions whose output has genus greater than
𝑔 are no longer defined. More concretely, we say
                                        𝑥◦𝑗𝑖 𝑦       and         𝜉𝑖𝑗 𝑤
is undefined whenever
      ∙ 𝑥 ∈ (ℎ1 , 𝑚1 + 1) and 𝑦 ∈ (ℎ2 , 𝑚2 + 1) with ℎ1 + ℎ2 > 𝑔, or
      ∙ 𝑤 ∈ (ℎ, 𝑚 + 1) with ℎ ≥ 𝑔, so that 𝜉𝑖𝑗 𝑤 ∈ (ℎ + 1, 𝑚 − 1) has genus greater than 𝑔.
We write Mod≤𝑔 (ℰ ) for the category of genus-𝑔 truncated modular operads in ℰ . The truncation
construction defines a functor
                                   tr ∗≤𝑔 ∶ Mod(ℰ ) ⟶ Mod≤𝑔 (ℰ ),
which admits both a left adjoint and a right adjoint:
                                                 (tr ≤𝑔 )!


                                     Mod(ℰ )                 Mod≤𝑔 (ℰ ).
                                                  tr ∗≤𝑔

                                                 (tr ≤𝑔 )∗


Proposition 2.25. The category of cyclic operads is naturally equivalent to the category of genus-0
truncated modular operads. Moreover, the truncation functor
                                     tr ∗≤0 ∶ Mod(ℰ ) ⟶ Cyc(ℰ )
preserves weak equivalences and is the right adjoint in a Quillen adjunction.

Similarly, there is a forgetful functor 𝑢∗ ∶ Cyc(ℰ ) → Op(ℰ ) which forgets the cyclic structure. This
functor admits a left adjoint, called the cyclic envelope:
                                                     𝑢!
                                       Op(ℰ )                Cyc(ℰ ).
                                                    𝑢∗
An explicit description of 𝑢! is somewhat involved; see [19, Section 3.1] or [61, Section 9]. The
forgetful functor 𝑢∗ preserves weak equivalences, and the adjunction above is a Quillen adjunction.
2.6. Mapping Class Groups. A surface of type (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) is a compact, oriented surface 𝑆 of genus
𝑔 with 𝑛 + 1 boundary components.

Definition 2.26. A labelled surface of type (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) is a pair (𝑆, 𝛿), where
                                                             ≅
                                    𝛿 ∶ {0, … , 𝑛}               𝜋0 (𝜕𝑆)
is a labelling of the boundary components, i.e. we write 𝜕𝑖 𝑆 ∶= 𝛿(𝑖) for the 𝑖th boundary component.
A parameterized labelled surface (𝑆, 𝛿, {𝑥𝑖 }) consists of a labelled surface together with a choice of
point 𝑥𝑖 ∈ 𝜕𝑖 𝑆 for each 0 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑛.

Definition 2.27. Let 𝑆 be a surface of type (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1). The associated pure mapping class group is
                                      Γ(𝑆)∶ = 𝜋0 Dif f + (𝑆; 𝜕𝑆),
where Dif f + (𝑆; 𝜕𝑆) denotes the group of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms of 𝑆 that fix 𝜕𝑆
pointwise. Elements of the mapping class group Γ(𝑆) are called mapping classes. The group structure
on Γ(𝑆) is induced by composition, and we use the usual order of composition of functions when
writing the group multiplication.
16                               L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


Any two surfaces of type (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) have isomorphic pure mapping class groups and we denote this
group by Γ𝑔,𝑛+1 .

Remark 2.28. More generally, a mapping class may refer to the isotopy class of a diffeomorphism
between different manifolds that is not required to fix the boundary pointwise. Mapping classes that
do fix the boundary pointwise are often called pure mapping classes. Since all but one of the mapping
classes considered in this paper (see Figure 4b) fix the boundary pointwise, we will use the term
mapping class to mean pure mapping class.

2.6.1. Presentations of mapping class groups. Mapping class groups of orientable surfaces are well
understood, and several presentations are available [27, 40, 36, 60]. We highlight two cases that will
play an important role throughout the paper. For a comprehensive reference, see [23].

Example 2.29. The mapping class group of the disc is trivial, i.e. Γ(𝐷2 ) =∗. This is known as
Alexander’s lemma [23, Lemma 2.1].

We now recall the mapping classes that are used as standard generators for higher genus surfaces.
Throughout this paper, we use the terms curve and loop to mean the isotopy class of an embedded
closed curve in 𝑆. The geometric intersection number |𝑎 ∩ 𝑏| of two curves 𝑎 and 𝑏 is the minimum
number of intersection points among all representatives in their isotopy classes. Similarly, the self-
intersection number of a curve is the minimum self-intersection number among all representatives. A
simple loop is a loop of self-intersection number 0.
A fundamental family of mapping classes is given by Dehn twists about simple loops. We denote the
Dehn twist about a simple loop 𝑎 by 𝐷𝑎 . See [23, Section 4.4] for details.

Proposition 2.30. [26, 45] For a surface 𝑆 of type (𝑔, 𝑛+1) the mapping class group Γ(𝑆) is generated
by all Dehn twists along simple closed curves together with the following relations:
     (C) If |𝑎 ∩ 𝑏| = 0, then 𝐷𝑎 𝐷𝑏 = 𝐷𝑏 𝐷𝑎 .
     (B) If |𝑎 ∩ 𝑏| = 1 and 𝑐 = 𝐷𝑏 (𝑎), then 𝐷𝑐 = 𝐷𝑏 𝐷𝑎 𝐷𝑏−1 .
         Relation (B) implies the braid relation 𝐷𝑎 𝐷𝑏 𝐷𝑎 = 𝐷𝑏 𝐷𝑎 𝐷𝑏 , if |𝑎 ∩ 𝑏| = 1.
     (D) The doughnut relation, (𝐷𝑎 𝐷𝑏 𝐷𝑎 )4 = 𝐷𝜕 which takes place on a subsurface of 𝑆 of type
         (1, 1), 𝑎 and 𝑏 are the curves represented in Figure 1a, and 𝐷𝜕 denotes the twist around the
         boundary.
     (L) The lantern relation, 𝐷𝜕0 𝐷𝜕1 𝐷𝜕2 𝐷𝜕3 = 𝐷𝑏1 𝐷𝑏2 𝐷𝑏3 , where the 𝜕𝑖 are loops in 𝑆 bounding a
         subsurface of type (0, 4) and the 𝑏𝑗 are as in Figure 1b.

2.6.2. A diagrammatic representation of mapping classes. The goal of this subsection is to give a
concrete combinatorial model for mapping classes in genus 0. More precisely, we describe how iso-
topy classes of boundary-fixing diffeomorphisms can be encoded by embedded trees. This provides
the diagrammatic framework needed later to give a presentation of our modular operad.
Let (𝑆, 𝛿, {𝑥𝑖 }𝑛𝑖=0 ) and (𝑆 ′ , 𝛿 ′ , {𝑥′𝑖 }𝑛𝑖=0 ) be parametrised labelled surfaces of type (0, 𝑛 + 1), each
equipped with a chosen point 𝑥𝑖 ∈ 𝜕𝑖 𝑆 and 𝑥′𝑖 ∈ 𝜕𝑖 𝑆 ′ on the 𝑖th boundary component. We write
       (          )
Dif f + 𝑆, 𝑆 ′ ; 𝜕 for the space of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms 𝜙 ∶ 𝑆 → 𝑆 ′ that preserve
the boundary labels and satisfy 𝜙(𝑥𝑖 ) = 𝑥′𝑖 for all 0 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑛. Its set of path components
                                                               (           )
                                                     𝜋0 Dif f + 𝑆, 𝑆 ′ ; 𝜕
is the set of isotopy classes of such diffeomorphisms. In genus 0 we will encode these isotopy classes
by combinatorial data coming from embedded trees.

Definition 2.32. Let 𝑇 be a tree with |𝜕(𝑇 )| = 𝑛 + 1 equipped with a boundary labelling
                                                                ≅
                                        𝓁𝑇 ∶ {0, 1, … , 𝑛}            𝜕(𝑇 )
Let (𝑆, 𝛿, {𝑥𝑖 }) be a labelled surface of type (0, 𝑛 + 1) with chosen points 𝑥𝑖 ∈ 𝜕𝑖 𝑆 for 0 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑛. A
marking of (𝑆, 𝛿, {𝑥𝑖 }) by 𝑇 is an equivalence class of embeddings 𝑚 ∶ 𝑇 ↪ 𝑆 such that
                              (      )
                            𝑚 𝓁𝑇 (𝑖) = 𝑥𝑖 ∈ 𝜕𝑖 𝑆,         for all 𝑖 ∈ {0, … , 𝑛},
where 𝑚 ∼ 𝑚′ if 𝑚′ is obtained from 𝑚 by an isotopy relative to 𝜕𝑆 or by precomposition with an
element of Homeo(𝑇 ).
                                      (                 )
A marked genus-zero surface is a pair (𝑆, 𝛿, {𝑥𝑖 }), [𝑚] consisting of a labelled genus-zero surface
(𝑆, 𝛿, {𝑥𝑖 }) and a marking 𝑚 ∶ 𝑇 ↪ 𝑆 by a boundary-labelled tree 𝑇 .

Any marked surface determines additional combinatorial structure on the underlying tree. Since 𝑇 is
embedded in the oriented surface 𝑆, the incident half-edges at each vertex inherit a cyclic ordering
from the orientation of 𝑆. A graph equipped with a cyclic ordering of the incident half-edges at each
vertex is called a ribbon graph, and the corresponding data is called a ribbon structure.
In addition, if 𝑈𝑇 is a sufficiently small tubular neighbourhood of 𝑇 in 𝑆, then 𝜕𝑈𝑇 is an oriented
circle, and the legs of 𝑇 meet 𝜕𝑈𝑇 in a cyclically ordered set. Via the boundary labelling 𝓁𝑇 , this
induces a cyclic order on the set of boundary labels {0, … , 𝑛}.

                               𝑆 ⧵ int(𝑈𝑇 )       and       𝑆 ′ ⧵ int(𝑈𝑇 ′ ),

be two representatives of the marking [𝑚′ ]. Then the diffeomorphisms obtained from the above con-
struction using (𝑚, 𝑚′ ) and (𝑚,

𝑆.′                                                                                                 □

2.7. The Complex of Markings. In the study of presentations of mapping class groups, it is standard
to consider a 2-dimensional CW complex whose vertices encode decompositions of a surface and
whose edges correspond to elementary moves between them. To give a presentation of our modular
operad of mapping class groups, we use a variant of the complex of maximal markings of [4, Section 5].
A cut system on a surface 𝑆 is a finite collection of pairwise disjoint simple loops 𝐶 = {𝑐1 , … , 𝑐𝑘 },
up to isotopy, such that the complement is a disjoint union of genus-zero punctured surfaces. Each
puncture can be canonically replaced by a boundary component, yielding a disjoint union of genus-
zero surfaces with boundary. By abuse of notation, we denote the resulting disjoint union of surfaces
by
                                                   ⋃        ∐
                                     𝑆 ⧵𝐶 =𝑆 ⧵         𝑐𝑖 =    𝑆𝑗 .
                                                                 𝑗
                                                        𝑖
Each surface 𝑆𝑗 is canonically associated to a subsurface of 𝑆, and we refer to the surfaces 𝑆𝑗 as the
cut-subsurfaces determined by 𝐶. See, for example, [36, 35, 49].
We will enhance a cut system by equipping the cut-subsurfaces with compatible markings. These
markings assemble into an embedding of a graph 𝐺 recording how the genus-zero pieces are con-
nected: the vertices of 𝐺 correspond to cut-subsurfaces, while the internal edges correspond to the
cut curves.
                                     GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                      19


Definition 2.34. [4, Definition 3.3] Let 𝑆 = (𝑆, 𝛿, {𝑥𝑖 }𝑛𝑖=0 ) be a labelled parameterized surface of
type (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) with 𝑛 ≥ 0. A marked cut system on 𝑆 consists of a pair (𝐶, [𝑚]), where:
      ∙ 𝐶 = {𝑐1 , … , 𝑐𝑘 } is a cut system on 𝑆;
      ∙ [𝑚] is an equivalence class of embeddings 𝑚 ∶ 𝐺 ↪ 𝑆, where 𝑚 ∼ 𝑚′ if 𝑚′ is obtained from
        𝑚 by an isotopy relative to 𝜕𝑆 or by precomposition with an element of Homeo(𝐺).
These data are required to satisfy the following conditions:
    (1) The embedding 𝑚 is compatible with the decomposition determined by 𝐶 in the following
        sense:
           ∙ the internal edges 𝑖𝐸𝐺 of 𝐺 are in bijection with the curves in 𝐶, and the image of each
             internal edge meets the corresponding curve transversely in a single point and is disjoint
             from all other curves in 𝐶;
           ∙ the image of each vertex lies in a distinct cut-subsurface of 𝑆, and this determines a
             bijection of the vertices 𝑉𝐺 with the connected components of 𝑆 ⧵ 𝐶;
           ∙ for each vertex 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉𝐺 , the half-edges incident to 𝑣 are in bijection with the boundary
             components of the corresponding cut-subsurface 𝑆𝑣 .
    (2) The embedding 𝑚 sends each leg of 𝐺 to a marked boundary point of 𝑆. Equivalently, for
        every leg 𝑒 ∈ 𝜕(𝐺) there exists 𝑖 ∈ {0, … , 𝑛} such that the endpoint of 𝑒 is mapped to
        𝑥𝑖 ∈ 𝛿(𝑖) ⊂ 𝜕𝑆.
    (3) For each vertex 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉𝐺 , the restriction of 𝑚 to the corolla subgraph 𝐶𝑣 ⊂ 𝐺 induces a marking
                                            𝑚𝑣 ∶ 𝐶𝑣 ↪ 𝑆𝑣
        in the sense of Definition 2.32, where 𝑆𝑣 is the cut-subsurface corresponding to 𝑣 under (1).
A marked cut system (𝐶, [𝑚]) is called a marked pants decomposition if every cut-subsurface 𝑆𝑗 is a
surface of type (0, 3).

Example 2.35. For the pair of pants 𝑃 , markings are represented by embeddings of the corolla 𝑚 ∶
𝐶3 ↪ 𝑃 . Figure 3 depicts one such marking, which we call the standard marking 𝑚𝑃 on 𝑃 .
The surfaces 𝑆 and 𝑆 are obtained by gluing two copies of 𝑃 in the two ways shown in Figure 3.
The same figure shows the corresponding marked pants decompositions, which we call the standard
markings on 𝑆 and 𝑆 .
The surface 𝑆 is obtained from a single copy of 𝑃 by gluing the boundary components 𝜕1 and 𝜕2 . Its
standard marking, also shown in Figure 3, is induced by gluing the corresponding half-edges of the
embedded corolla in 𝑃 .




                       F IGURE 3. Standard markings on 𝑃 , 𝑆 , 𝑆 , and 𝑆 .


The complex of markings is a CW complex whose vertices are marked cut systems. Its edges are
generated by the following mapping classes, written using the diagrammatic representation introduced
in Section 2.6.2.

We now define the CW complex of markings used throughout the paper. It is a variant of the complex
of maximal markings of [4, Section 5]. The main difference is that we restrict to markings over pants
decompositions, so the cut-subsurfaces are all pairs of pants. We refer to Appendix C for the full list
of 2-cell relations, and record below those most relevant for this paper.

2-cells. The 2-cells are attached along loops expressing relations among the 1-cells. Most of these
coincide with the relations in [4]; see also Appendix C.
In describing these relations, we use the following conventions. For 𝑓 ∈ Γ(𝑆) and a marking 𝑚 ∶
                                                                          𝑚      𝑓
𝐺 ↪ 𝑆, we write 𝑓 ◦𝑚 for the marking represented by 𝐺 ←←←→            ← 𝑆. For a subsurface 𝑆 ′ ⊂ 𝑆,
                                                               ← 𝑆 ←←←→
we write 𝑚|𝑆 ′ for the induced marking obtained by restricting the image of 𝑚 to the corresponding
subgraph. In particular, if 𝑆 ′ is a cut-subsurface, then 𝑚|𝑆 ′ is the corolla marking 𝑚𝑣 ∶ 𝐶𝑣 ↪ 𝑆 ′
associated to the corresponding vertex 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉𝐺 .
We include the following families of relations, which are described in detail in Appendix C:
     (1) commutativity of disjoint moves;
     (2) boundary twist relations;
     (3) pentagon relation;
     (4) two hexagon relations;
     (5) self-duality of associativity,
     (6) twist–braiding relation;
     (7) gluing relations for Dehn twists.

In addition to the local relations above, we impose the following genus-one relations. For surfaces of
type (1, 1), we impose

(G1.1)                                                       𝑆𝑆𝐵 = id,
(G1.2)                                    𝑇   −1
                                                   𝑆𝑇   −1
                                                             𝑆𝑇 −1 = 𝑆

shown in more detail in Figures 7a and 7b.
For surfaces of type (1, 2), we also impose the relation shown in Figure 8. This relation differs slightly
from the corresponding relation in [4].

The relations above are chosen so that the resulting complex has the expected connectivity properties.
The result we will use later is the following.

Proposition 2.38. Let 𝑆 = (𝑆, 𝛿, {𝑥𝑖 }𝑛𝑖=0 ) be a parameterized, labelled surface of type (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1),
with 𝑛 ≥ 0. Then the complex of markings over pants decompositions on 𝑆, (𝑆), is connected and
simply connected.

A proof is given in Appendix C.

                     3. A M ODULAR O PERAD OF M APPING C LASS G ROUPOIDS
In this section, we define a modular operad in groupoids 𝐒 = {𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛+1)}𝑔≥0, 𝑛≥0 , which homotopically
approximates a modular operad constructed from the mapping class groups in the sense that,
                                        B𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) ≃ BΓ𝑔,𝑛+1 .
In Section 3.1.1 we will compare each groupoid 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) with the corresponding complex of mark-
ings. This comparison will allow us to give a finite presentation of 𝐒 by generators and relations.
3.1. The Modular Operad 𝐒. Recall that we write Σ+        𝑛 = Aut(0, 1, … , 𝑛) for the extended symmetric
group. If 𝐶𝑛+1 is a corolla with (n+1) boundary legs, then a labelling of its boundary by the set
0, 1, … , 𝑛 is equivalent to a choice of element of Σ+
                                                     𝑛 . Indeed, once a reference labelling is fixed, every
other labelling is obtained by precomposition with a unique permutation in Σ+      𝑛.
24                               L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


In the case 𝑛 = 2, the alternating subgroup of Σ+
                                                2
                                                  = Aut({0, 1, 2}) is given by 𝐴+
                                                                                2
                                                                                  = {id, (012), (021)}.
The quotient Σ2 ∕𝐴2 has two elements, represented by
               +     +

                                     id ⋅𝐴+
                                          2
                                                    and        (12) ⋅ 𝐴+
                                                                       2
                                                                         .
The permutations in 𝐴+
                     2
                       preserve the cyclic ordering of the labels, so the three labellings in each coset
determine the same cyclic ordering of the boundary legs.

Definition 3.1. Let Υ = {Υ(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)} be the free modular operad in Set generated by the set
                                              Υ(0, 3) = Σ+
                                                         2
                                                           ∕𝐴+
                                                             2
                                                               .
The set Υ(0, 3) consists of the two planar trivalent corollas with boundary labelled by {0, 1, 2}, which
we denote by
                              𝑃 = id ⋅𝐴+
                                       2

                                                                         2

Following Definition 2.24, we see that an element of Υ(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) is an isomorphism class of connected
decorated graphs 𝐺 = (𝐺, 𝜆, 𝓁𝐺 , 𝜖, 𝜐) where:
     (1) every vertex 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉𝐺 is trivalent and has genus 𝜖(𝑣) = 0;
     (2) The vertex decoration 𝜐 = (𝜐𝜆(1) , … , 𝜐𝜆(𝑘) ) consists of a sequence of elements of Υ(0, 3).
         In other words, each vertex 𝑣𝜆(𝑖) is decorated by one of the two planar trivalent corollas in
         Υ(0, 3), equivalently by a bijection
                                                           ≅
                                      𝓁𝑣𝜆(𝑖) ∶ {0, 1, 2} ←←←→
                                                            ← 𝑛𝑏(𝑣𝜆(𝑖) )
         defined up to the action of 𝐴+
                                      2
                                        ;
     (3) the boundary of 𝐺 is equipped with a labelling
                                                             ≅
                                       𝓁𝐺 ∶ {0, … , 𝑛} ←←←→
                                                          ← 𝜕(𝐺);

     (4) the total genus of 𝐺 is 𝑔, equivalently 𝛽1 (𝐺) = 𝑔.
Condition (2) is equivalent to equipping each vertex of 𝐺 with a cyclic ordering of its incident half-
edges. In other words, 𝐺 is a ribbon graph, and the isomorphism classes above are isomorphism
classes of ribbon graphs. Accordingly, we will often regard elements of Υ(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) as ribbon graphs
with first Betti number 𝑔 and 𝑛 + 1 ordered legs. However, the interpretation of Υ(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) as the free
modular operad generated by a symmetric sequence will be essential in later sections.

Our next goal is to define, for each 𝑔, 𝑛 ≥ 0 satisfying 2𝑔 + 𝑛 ≥ 1, a groupoid 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) whose objects
are the decorated graphs in Υ(𝑔, 𝑛+1). The construction is arranged so that, for every 𝐺 ∈ Υ(𝑔, 𝑛+1),
there is a natural isomorphism
                                         Aut 𝐒(𝑔,𝑛+1) (𝐺) ≅ Γ(𝑆𝐺 ),
where 𝑆𝐺 is the surface associated to 𝐺.
We now describe this surface. Let 𝐺 = (𝐺, 𝜆, 𝓁𝐺 , 𝜖, 𝜐) ∈ Υ(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) be a decorated graph. From 𝐺
