        Together this defines a marked pants decomposition (𝑖𝐸𝐺 , [𝑚𝐺 ]) of the surface 𝑆𝐺 .

Definition 3.2. We define a family of groupoids 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1), for 𝑔, 𝑛 ≥ 0 satisfying 2𝑔 + 𝑛 ≥ 1, as
follows.
For (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) = (0, 2), let 𝐒(0, 2) be the groupoid with a single object | and
                                               Hom𝐒(0,2) (|, |) = ℤ.
We regard this as the groupoid version of the mapping class group of a thin cylinder, whose morphisms
correspond to Dehn twists along the boundary; e.g. [59, Section 3.1.1].

For every other pair 𝑔, 𝑛 ≥ 0 satisfying 2𝑔 + 𝑛 ≥ 1, the objects of 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) are the decorated graphs
in Υ(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1):
                                     ob(𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)) = Υ(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1).
Given 𝐺, 𝐺′ ∈ Υ(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1), define
                               Hom𝐒(𝑔,𝑛+1) (𝐺, 𝐺′ ) ∶= 𝜋0 Dif f (𝑆𝐺 , 𝑆𝐺′ ; 𝜕),
26                               L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


where the diffeomorphisms preserve the boundary labelling and send the marked points on the bound-
ary of 𝑆𝐺 to the corresponding marked points on the boundary of 𝑆𝐺′ . Composition is induced by
composition of diffeomorphisms.

The group Σ+ 1
               acts trivially on the exceptional entry 𝐒(0, 2). On every other entry 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1), the
extended symmetric group Σ+    𝑛 acts by permuting the labels on the boundary legs of the underlying
decorated graphs. Thus
                                     𝐒 = {𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)}𝑔,𝑛≥0,2𝑔+𝑛≥1
is a genus-graded sequence in groupoids.
We now define the modular operad structure. For 𝑛 + 𝑚 ≥ 1 and boundary labels 0 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑛 and
0 ≤ 𝑗 ≤ 𝑚, the composition functor
                          ◦𝑗𝑖 ∶ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) × 𝐒(ℎ, 𝑚 + 1) ⟶ 𝐒(𝑔 + ℎ, 𝑛 + 𝑚)
is defined on objects by grafting decorated graphs, so that (𝐺, 𝐻) is sent to 𝐺◦𝑗𝑖 𝐻, as in Definition 2.24.
The unique object | ∈ 𝐒(0, 2) is the unit for graph grafting. The generating morphism 1 ∈ ℤ =
Hom𝐒(0,2) (|, |) acts by Dehn twists: composing it at the 𝑖th input with a morphism [𝜙] ∶ 𝑆𝐺 → 𝑆𝐻
produces the mapping class [𝐷𝜕𝑖 ◦𝜙] ∶ 𝑆𝐺 → 𝑆𝐻 , where 𝜕𝑖 denotes the 𝑖th boundary component of
𝑆𝐻 .
For all other pairs (𝑔, 𝑛), the associated surface 𝑆𝐺◦𝑗 𝐻 is obtained by gluing 𝑆𝐺 and 𝑆𝐻 along the
                                                      𝑖
boundary components labelled 𝓁𝐺 (𝑖) and 𝓁𝐻 (𝑗). The same gluing operation defines the functor on
morphisms. Namely, if 𝜙 ∶ 𝑆𝐺 → 𝑆𝐺′ and 𝜓 ∶ 𝑆𝐻 → 𝑆𝐻 ′ are boundary-label-preserving diffeomor-
phisms, then they carry the chosen boundary components to the corresponding boundary components
of 𝑆𝐺′ and 𝑆𝐻 ′ . Hence they glue to a diffeomorphism 𝜙◦𝑗𝑖 𝜓 ∶ 𝑆𝐺◦𝑗 𝐻 ⟶ 𝑆𝐺′ ◦𝑗 𝐻 ′ . This construction
                                                                    𝑖           𝑖
is compatible with isotopy classes, and therefore defines the desired functor.
Similarly, for 𝑛 ≥ 2 and distinct labels 0 ≤ 𝑖, 𝑗 ≤ 𝑛, the contraction functor
                                  𝜉𝑖𝑗 ∶ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) ⟶ 𝐒(𝑔 + 1, 𝑛 − 1)
is defined on objects by grafting together the two boundary legs of 𝐺 labelled 𝓁𝐺 (𝑖) and 𝓁𝐺 (𝑗). On
surfaces, this amounts to gluing the corresponding boundary components of 𝑆𝐺 , thereby increasing
the genus by one and reducing the number of boundary components by two.
On morphisms, a boundary-label-preserving diffeomorphism 𝜙 ∶ 𝑆𝐺 → 𝑆𝐺′ induces, after gluing,
a diffeomorphism 𝑆𝜉 𝑗 𝐺 → 𝑆𝜉 𝑗 𝐺′ . This construction is compatible with isotopy classes and hence
                      𝑖         𝑖
defines the contraction functor.

Definition 3.3. The modular operad of surfaces,
                                        𝐒 = {𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)}2𝑔+𝑛≥1 ,
is the modular operad in groupoids whose (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)-component is the groupoid 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) of genus 𝑔
surfaces with 𝑛 + 1 marked boundary components. The modular operad structure is induced by gluing
boundary components, or equivalently by the grafting and contraction operations on graphs.

The cyclic operad of genus-zero surfaces, denoted tr ≤0 𝐒, is obtained by restricting 𝐒 to genus zero.
Its component with 𝑛 + 1 boundary components is
                                       tr ≤0 𝐒(𝑛 + 1) = 𝐒(0, 𝑛 + 1).
The cyclic operad structure is induced by the gluing operations in 𝐒 which glue distinct genus-zero
surfaces along boundary components. On markings, these operations correspond to grafting the un-
derlying labelled trees.

By construction, for every object 𝐺 ∈ ob(𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)), the automorphism group of 𝐺 is the mapping
class group of the associated surface:
                                   Aut 𝐒(𝑔,𝑛+1) (𝐺) = Γ(𝑆𝐺 ) ≅ Γ𝑔,𝑛+1 .
                                      GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                      27


Since 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) is a connected groupoid, the following lemma follows immediately.

Lemma 3.4. For each 𝑔 ≥ 0 and 𝑛 ≥ 0 satisfying 2𝑔 + 𝑛 ≥ 1, there is a weak equivalence
                                       B𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) ≃ BΓ𝑔,𝑛+1 .

Remark 3.5. The modular operad in Definition 3.3 is closely related to the surface operads of [57,
Construction 2.2] and [59, Section 3.1]. These constructions all build surfaces by gluing basic pieces
along boundary components, but they differ in which pieces are taken as “atomic”. In the constructions
of Tillmann and Wahl, the atomic surfaces include the pair of pants, the one-holed torus, and the disc.
In our setting, the pair of pants is the only atomic surface.

3.1.1. A presentation of the modular operad 𝐒. Fix an object 𝐺 ∈ ob(𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)), regarded as a
decorated graph, and let 𝑆𝐺 denote the associated surface equipped with its standard marked pants
decomposition
                                      (𝑖𝐸𝐺 , [𝑚𝐺 ] ∶ 𝐺 ↪ 𝑆𝐺 ).
We compare the topological encoding of markings of 𝑆𝐺 , given by the complex of markings, with the
categorical encoding given by the coslice category 𝐺 ↓ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1).
Recall the complex of markings (𝑆𝐺 ) from Definition 2.37. We write 𝕄𝐺 for the groupoid presented
by this 2-complex. Its objects are the 0-cells of (𝑆𝐺 ), its generating morphisms are the edges of
(𝑆𝐺 ), and its relations are the boundaries of the 2-cells. Equivalently, Hom𝕄𝐺 (𝑣, 𝑤) consists of
homotopy classes, relative endpoints, of edge paths from 𝑣 to 𝑤.

Definition 3.6. Fix a graph 𝐺 ∈ ob(𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)). The coslice category 𝐺 ↓ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) is the category
whose objects are morphisms 𝜙 ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻 in 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1), as 𝐻 ranges over the objects of 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1).
A morphism from 𝜙1 ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻1 to 𝜙2 ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻2 is a morphism 𝜓 ∶ 𝐻1 → 𝐻2 in 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) such
that 𝜓𝜙1 = 𝜙2 . In other words, a morphism from 𝜙1 → 𝜙2 is a morphism 𝜓 ∶ 𝐻1 → 𝐻2 which
makes the diagram
                                              𝐺
                                            𝜙1           𝜙2


                                       𝐻1          𝜓          𝐻2
commute.

Lemma 3.7. Fix a graph 𝐺 ∈ ob(𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)) and let 𝑆𝐺 be the associated labelled, parametrized
surface. Then the coslice category 𝐺 ↓ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) is isomorphic to the groupoid 𝕄𝐺 .

Proof. We first construct a functor 𝐼 ∶ 𝕄𝐺 ⟶ 𝐺 ↓ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) and then show that it is an isomor-
phism.
To define 𝐼 on objects, let (𝐶, [𝑚] ∶ 𝐻 ↪ 𝑆𝐺 ) be a vertex of the complex of markings (𝑆𝐺 ). Since
𝐶 is a pants decomposition, the components of 𝑆𝐺 ⧵ 𝐶 are pairs of pants. Thus the graph 𝐻 is trivalent,
with internal edges corresponding to the curves of 𝐶 and vertices corresponding to the pair-of-pants
components. Since every pants decomposition of a surface of type (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) has 2𝑔 + 𝑛 − 1 = |𝜒(𝑆𝐺 )|
pairs of pants, the graph 𝐻 has 2𝑔 + 𝑛 − 1 trivalent vertices. An Euler characteristic argument then
shows that 𝛽1 (𝐻) = 𝑔.
The marking [𝑚] determines the additional structure required to regard 𝐻 as an object of 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1).
Indeed, the orientation of 𝑆𝐺 induces a cyclic ordering on the half-edges incident to each vertex, while
the labelling of the boundary components of 𝑆𝐺 induces a labelling of the boundary legs of 𝐻. Thus
the marked pants decomposition determines an object of 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1), which we again denote by 𝐻.
The object 𝐻 comes with its standard surface 𝑆𝐻 and standard marked pants decomposition (𝐶𝐻 , [𝑚𝐻 ]).
By construction, the marked pants decomposition (𝐶, [𝑚]) of 𝑆𝐺 and the standard marked pants de-
composition of 𝑆𝐻 have the same associated graph. Therefore the local identifications of the corre-
sponding pairs of pants glue to a boundary-label-preserving diffeomorphism 𝜙 ∶ 𝑆𝐺 ⟶ 𝑆𝐻 , which
28                              L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


is well defined up to isotopy. We set
                                        𝐼(𝐶, [𝑚]) = [𝜙 ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻],
viewed as an object of the coslice category 𝐺 ↓ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1).

We now define 𝐼 on morphisms. Let 𝛼 ∶ (𝐶, [𝑚]) ⟶ (𝐶 ′ , [𝑚′ ]) be a morphism in 𝕄𝐺 , and suppose
               𝐼(𝐶, [𝑚]) = [𝜙 ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻]           and       𝐼(𝐶 ′ , [𝑚′ ]) = [𝜙′ ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻 ′ ].
There is a unique morphism in the coslice category from [𝜙] to [𝜙′ ], namely the mapping class
                                          [𝜙′ 𝜙−1 ] ∶ 𝐻 ⟶ 𝐻 ′ .
We set 𝐼(𝛼) = [𝜙′ 𝜙−1 ].

It remains to show that 𝐼 is an isomorphism. On objects, 𝐼 is bijective. Indeed, given an object
[𝜙] ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻 of 𝐺 ↓ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1), one can pull back the standard marked pants decomposition
(𝐶𝐻 , [𝑚𝐻 ]) of 𝑆𝐻 along 𝜙 to define a marked pants decomposition
                                          (𝜙−1 (𝐶𝐻 ), [𝜙−1 𝑚𝐻 ])
of 𝑆𝐺 . This construction is inverse to the one above.
For morphisms, first observe that the coslice category 𝐺 ↓ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) has exactly one morphism
between any two objects. Indeed, if the objects are [𝜙] ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻 and [𝜙′ ] ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻 ′ , then a
morphism between them is a morphism [𝜓] ∶ 𝐻 → 𝐻 ′ satisfying [𝜓]◦[𝜙] = [𝜙′ ]. Since 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)
is a groupoid, [𝜙] is invertible, so [𝜓] is necessarily [𝜙′ ]◦[𝜙]−1 .
On the other hand, 𝕄𝐺 is the fundamental groupoid of (𝑆𝐺 ) on its vertices. Since (𝑆𝐺 ) is con-
nected and simply connected by Proposition 2.38, there is exactly one homotopy class of paths between
any two vertices. Thus 𝕄𝐺 also has exactly one morphism between any two objects. Therefore 𝐼 is
bijective on all Hom-sets. Hence 𝐼 is an isomorphism of categories. It follows that
                                         𝕄𝐺 ≅ 𝐺 ↓ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1).
                                                                                                   □


Recall the mapping classes that described the standard 𝑇 -, 𝐵-, 𝐴-, and 𝑆-moves in Definition 2.36.
We write
                                     (           )                  (    )                  ( )
 𝜏 ∈ Hom𝐒(0,2) (|, |), 𝛽 ∈ Hom𝐒(0,3) , (12)∗ , 𝛼 ∈ Hom𝐒(0,4) , , 𝜎 ∈ Hom𝐒(1,1) ,
for the corresponding morphisms in 𝐒.

Proposition 3.8. Every morphism in 𝐒 can be obtained from 𝜏, 𝛽, 𝛼, 𝜎, and their inverses using
categorical composition, operadic composition, symmetric group actions, and contractions.

Proof. Let [𝜙] ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻 be a morphism in 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1), represented by a boundary-label-preserving
diffeomorphism
                                    𝜙 ∶ 𝑆𝐺 ⟶ 𝑆𝐻 .
By Lemma 3.7, this morphism corresponds to the marked pants decomposition of 𝑆𝐺 obtained by
pulling back the standard marked pants decomposition of 𝑆𝐻 along 𝜙.
We first translate a single edge of the complex of markings into a morphism of 𝐒. Suppose that
                                          (𝐶, [𝑚]) ⇝ (𝐶 ′ , [𝑚′ ])
is an edge of (𝑆𝐺 ) of type 𝑇 , 𝐵, 𝐴, or 𝑆. Let
                             [𝜙] ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻           and       [𝜓] ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻 ′
                                       GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                        29


be the objects of the coslice category 𝐺 ↓ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) corresponding to (𝐶, [𝑚]) and (𝐶 ′ , [𝑚′ ]), respec-
tively, under Lemma 3.7. The edge determines the unique morphism in the coslice category from [𝜙]
to [𝜓]; equivalently, there is a unique morphism [𝑓 ] ∶ 𝐻 → 𝐻 ′ such that
                                              [𝜓] = [𝑓 ]◦[𝜙].
We claim that [𝑓 ] is obtained from identities and one of the standard generators 𝜏, 𝛽, 𝛼, 𝜎 by operadic
composition, symmetric group actions, and contractions.
We verify the claim for a 𝐵-move; the other cases are analogous. Suppose that
                                                  𝐵𝑎,𝑏,𝑐
                            (𝐶, [𝑚] ∶ 𝐻 ↪ 𝑆𝐺 ) ⇝ (𝐶 ′ , [𝑚′ ] ∶ 𝐻 ′ ↪ 𝑆𝐺 )
is a 𝐵-move. Then 𝐶 = 𝐶 ′ , so the two marked pants decompositions have the same underlying cut
system. The move is supported on a single cut-subsurface

                                               𝑆 ′ ⊂ 𝑆𝐺 ⧵ 𝐶
with boundary components 𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐, and the markings 𝑚 and 𝑚′ agree on the complement of 𝑆 ′ .
                                   ≅
Choose an identification 𝜉 ∶ 𝑆 ′ ←←←→
                                    ← 𝑃 with the standard pair of pants such that
                     𝜉◦𝑚|𝑆 ′ = 𝑚𝑃 ,       𝜉(𝑎) = 𝜕1 ,      𝜉(𝑏) = 𝜕2 ,      𝜉(𝑐) = 𝜕0 .
By the definition of the 𝐵-move, under this identification the new marking is given by
                                          𝜉◦𝑚′ |𝑆 ′ = 𝛽◦𝜉◦𝑚|𝑆 ′ .

Let 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉 (𝐻) and 𝑣′ ∈ 𝑉 (𝐻 ′ ) be the vertices corresponding to the component 𝑆 ′ . Since the move
is supported on 𝑆 ′ , the graphs 𝐻 and 𝐻 ′ agree away from these vertices, with all boundary labels
preserved. We therefore define a mapping class
                                            𝑓 ∶ 𝑆𝐻 ⟶ 𝑆𝐻 ′
by taking it to be the identity on every pair-of-pants component except the one corresponding to 𝑣,
and by taking it on that component to be the standard 𝐵-move 𝛽 under the above identifications. If the
local identification with the standard pair of pants differs by a cyclic relabelling, then the local map is
the corresponding cyclic translate of 𝛽.
Thus [𝑓 ] ∶ 𝐻 → 𝐻 ′ is obtained from 𝛽 by operadic composition with identities, together with the
relevant symmetric group action. Moreover, the marking corresponding to [𝜓] is exactly the marking
obtained from the one corresponding to [𝜙] by applying [𝑓 ] after [𝜙]. Hence
                                              [𝜓] = [𝑓 ]◦[𝜙].

We now complete the proof. Let [𝜙] ∈ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)(𝐺, 𝐻), and let (𝐶𝜙 , [𝑚𝜙 ]) be the corresponding
marked pants decomposition of 𝑆𝐺 . Since (𝑆𝐺 ) is connected, there is an edge path
                              (𝐶0 , [𝑚0 ]) ⇝ (𝐶1 , [𝑚1 ]) ⇝ ⋯ ⇝ (𝐶𝑘 , [𝑚𝑘 ])
from the standard marking (𝐶0 , [𝑚0 ]) = (𝑖𝐸𝐺 , [𝑚𝐺 ]) to (𝐶𝑘 , [𝑚𝑘 ]) = (𝐶𝜙 , [𝑚𝜙 ]).
Under Lemma 3.7, the standard marking corresponds to the identity morphism id𝐺 ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐺. By the
single-edge argument above, each edge in the path corresponds to postcomposition with a morphism
                                            [𝑓𝑖 ] ∶ 𝐻𝑖−1 → 𝐻𝑖
obtained from identities and one of 𝜏, 𝛽, 𝛼, 𝜎 by operadic composition, symmetric group actions, and
contractions. Hence
                                     [𝜙] = [𝑓𝑘 ]◦ ⋯ ◦[𝑓1 ]◦ id𝐺 .
Thus [𝜙] lies in the subcategory generated by 𝜏, 𝛽, 𝛼, 𝜎 under categorical composition, operadic com-
position, symmetric group actions, and contractions. Since [𝜙] was arbitrary, the claim follows. □
30                                  L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


The preceding proposition identifies the generators of the morphisms in 𝐒. The relations among these
generators are encoded by the 2-cells of the complex of markings. Fix 𝐺 ∈ ob(𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)). A loop in
(𝑆𝐺 ) based at the standard marking (𝑖𝐸𝐺 , [𝑚𝐺 ]) is represented by an edge path
                                            𝑒1                 𝑒2         𝑒𝑘
                              (𝑖𝐸𝐺 , [𝑚𝐺 ]) ⇝ (𝐶1 , [𝑚1 ]) ⇝ ⋯ ⇝ (𝑖𝐸𝐺 , [𝑚𝐺 ]).
Under Lemma 3.7, this loop corresponds to a sequence of morphisms in 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) whose composite
is the identity morphism of 𝐺:
                                [𝑓𝑘 ]◦ ⋯ ◦[𝑓1 ] = id𝐺           in Aut 𝐒(𝑔,𝑛+1) (𝐺).
Each edge 𝑒𝑖 is one of the elementary moves 𝑇 , 𝐵, 𝐴, or 𝑆, so each [𝑓𝑖 ] is obtained from 𝜏, 𝛽, 𝛼, 𝜎 by
operadic composition, symmetric group actions, and contractions.
The boundaries of the 2-cells in (𝑆𝐺 ) therefore give equations among the generators 𝜏, 𝛽, 𝛼, 𝜎. The
goal of the next few sections is to show that these are the only equations satisfied by these generators.
This will give a presentation of 𝐒, and hence a classification of maps out of the modular operad 𝐒.
Before proving this in full generality, we first study the genus-zero part of 𝐒 and relate it to operads
whose presentations are already known.
3.2. Genus-zero surfaces and cyclic ribbon braids. As a first step toward a presentation of the
modular operad of surfaces, we study its genus-zero truncation. We use the known presentation of
the operad of parenthesized ribbon braids to obtain a presentation of tr ≤0 𝐒, and then show that this
identification is compatible with the cyclic structures.
3.2.1. Braids and ribbon braids. For 𝑛 ≥ 1, let 𝖡𝑛 denote the braid group on 𝑛 strands. It may
be defined as the fundamental group of the configuration space of 𝑛 unordered distinct points in the
complex plane. It admits the standard presentation
                     ⟨             |                                                       ⟩
               𝖡𝑛 = 𝛽1 , … , 𝛽𝑛−1 |𝛽𝑖 𝛽𝑗 = 𝛽𝑗 𝛽𝑖 for |𝑖 − 𝑗| ≥ 2, 𝛽𝑖 𝛽𝑖+1 𝛽𝑖 = 𝛽𝑖+1 𝛽𝑖 𝛽𝑖+1 .
                                   |
For each 𝑛 ≥ 1, there is a short exact sequence
                                                                    𝜋
                                1         𝖯𝖡𝑛           𝖡𝑛                 Σ𝑛               1,
where 𝜋 sends the braid generator 𝛽𝑖 to the transposition (𝑖 𝑖 + 1). The kernel of 𝜋 is the pure braid
group, denoted 𝖯𝖡𝑛 . It is generated by the elements
                                                   −1
                            𝑥𝑖𝑗 = 𝛽𝑗−1 ⋯ 𝛽𝑖+1 𝛽𝑖2 𝛽𝑖+1    −1
                                                       ⋯ 𝛽𝑗−1 ,                1 ≤ 𝑖 < 𝑗 ≤ 𝑛.

The ribbon braid group on 𝑛 strands, denoted 𝖱𝖡𝑛 , may be defined as the fundamental group of the
configuration space of 𝑛 unordered distinct points in the plane, each equipped with a framing (equiva-
lently, each equipped with a point of 𝑆 1 ). It is generated by the braid generators 𝛽1 , … , 𝛽𝑛−1 together
with twists 𝜏1 , … , 𝜏𝑛 , subject to the braid relations above and the additional relations
     𝛽𝑖 𝜏𝑗 = 𝜏𝑗 𝛽𝑖   (𝑗 ∉ {𝑖, 𝑖 + 1}),     𝛽𝑖 𝜏𝑖 = 𝜏𝑖+1 𝛽𝑖 ,            𝛽𝑖 𝜏𝑖+1 = 𝜏𝑖 𝛽𝑖 ,        𝜏𝑖 𝜏𝑗 = 𝜏𝑗 𝜏𝑖   (𝑖 ≠ 𝑗).
The pure ribbon braid group, denoted 𝖯𝖱𝖡𝑛 , is the kernel of the natural projection
                                                  𝖱𝖡𝑛 ⟶ Σ𝑛 .

The ribbon braid groups are closely related to mapping class groups of genus-zero surfaces. Indeed,
there is a natural homomorphism
                                          𝖯𝖱𝖡𝑛 ⟶ Γ0,𝑛+1
sending the pure braid generator 𝑥𝑖𝑗 to the Dehn twist about the simple closed curve separating the
boundary components labelled 𝑖 and 𝑗 from the remaining boundary components, and sending the twist
𝜏𝑘 to the full Dehn twist about the 𝑘th boundary component. The following proposition is standard,
cf., for example, [58, Section 1.5].

Proposition 3.9. There is an isomorphism of groups
                                                 𝖯𝖱𝖡𝑛 ≅ Γ0,𝑛+1 .
                                          GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                        31


3.2.2. The operad of parenthesized ribbon braids. The objects of the operad of parenthesized ribbon
braids are parenthesized binary products. We encode these products by planar rooted binary trees.

Definition 3.10. The magma operad Ω = {Ω(𝑛)}𝑛≥1 is the operad in Set whose elements are isomor-
phism classes of planar rooted binary trees with 𝑛 input legs, equipped with a labelling
                                                                  ≅
                                          𝓁𝑇 ∶ {0, 1, … , 𝑛} ←←←→
                                                                ← 𝜕(𝑇 ),
where 𝓁𝑇 (0) is the root.
Equivalently, Ω is the free operad generated by the two rooted binary corollas
                               𝜇 = 𝜇(0; 1, 2)       and       (12)∗ 𝜇 = 𝜇(0; 2, 1).
These are the two corollas obtained by decorating the rooted binary corolla by the two elements of
Σ2 , or equivalently by choosing the order of the two input legs relative to the fixed root. Operadic
composition is given by grafting the root of one tree onto an input leg of another. For further details,
see [24, Section 6.1] and [13, Definition 6.10].

Remark 3.11. Elements of Ω(𝑛) may be identified with fully parenthesized words in the symbols
{1, … , 𝑛}. Under this identification, the two generators 𝜇 and (12)∗ 𝜇 correspond to the binary words
(12) and (21), respectively, and every parenthesized word is obtained from these generators by operadic
composition. For example,
                            (((12)3)4),         ((32)(14))        and      (1((23)4))
are elements of Ω(4).

Definition 3.12. For each 𝑛 ≥ 1, let 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(𝑛) be the groupoid whose objects are the elements of Ω(𝑛).
Given two objects 𝑇1 , 𝑇2 ∈ Ω(𝑛), their boundary labellings determine a unique bijection
                                                              ≅
                                            𝜌𝑇1 ,𝑇2 ∶ 𝜕(𝑇1 ) ←←←→
                                                                ← 𝜕(𝑇2 )
such that 𝜌𝑇1 ,𝑇2 ◦𝓁𝑇1 = 𝓁𝑇2 . A morphism 𝑟 ∈ Hom𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(𝑛) (𝑇1 , 𝑇2 ) is a ribbon braid 𝑟 ∈ 𝖱𝖡𝑛 whose
underlying permutation is the permutation of the input legs induced by 𝜌𝑇1 ,𝑇2 .

Composition in 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(𝑛) is given by multiplication in the ribbon braid group 𝖱𝖡𝑛 . In particular, for any
object 𝑇 ∈ Ω(𝑛), the endomorphism group is naturally identified with the pure ribbon braid group:
                                            Aut 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(𝑛) (𝑇 ) ≅ 𝖯𝖱𝖡𝑛 .
The symmetric group Σ𝑛 acts on 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(𝑛) by permuting the labels of the input legs. The resulting
symmetric sequence
                                   𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡 = {𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(𝑛)}𝑛≥1
forms an operad in groupoids. On objects, operadic composition is the grafting operation in Ω. On
morphisms, it is given by inserting a ribbon braid in 𝖱𝖡𝑚 into the 𝑖th strand of a ribbon braid in 𝖱𝖡𝑛 .
See [13, Definition 6.11] for details.

An important feature of 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡 is that its morphisms are generated by three elementary morphisms:
      𝜏 ∈ Hom𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(1) (∣, ∣),       𝛽 ∈ Hom𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(2) (𝜇, (12)∗ 𝜇),            𝛼 ∈ Hom𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(3) (𝜇◦1 𝜇, 𝜇◦2 𝜇).
Here 𝜏 is the strand twist, 𝛽 is the braiding, and 𝛼 is the re-parenthesization isomorphism from 𝜇◦1 𝜇
to 𝜇◦2 𝜇. More precisely, every morphism in 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(𝑛) can be written as an iterated categorical and
operadic composite of these morphisms and their inverses; see [13, Section 7], following the general
framework of [24, Chapter 6]. This presentation allows one to classify maps out of 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡.
The following is a restatement of Lemmas 7.1 and 7.4 of [13], based on [24, Theorem 6.2.4]. We write
⋅ for categorical composition, read from right to left, and ◦𝑖 for operadic composition. Permutations
are written in cycle notation and act on the right.
32                                L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON

and

Recall from Proposition 3.8 that the genus-zero generating morphisms in 𝐒 are
                                               (           )
           𝜏 ∈ Hom𝐒(0,2) (|, |), 𝛽 ∈ Hom𝐒(0,3) , (12)∗       and 𝛼 ∈ Hom𝐒(0,4) ( , ),

The genus-zero truncation is naturally a cyclic operad, whose arity-(𝑛+1) component is tr ≤0 𝐒(𝑛+1) =
𝐒(0, 𝑛 + 1). Its underlying ordinary operad 𝑢∗ tr ≤0 𝐒 is obtained by choosing the boundary component
labelled 0 as the root, so that 𝑢∗ tr ≤0 𝐒(𝑛) = tr ≤0 𝐒(𝑛 + 1) = 𝐒(0, 𝑛 + 1). These are the same groupoids,
with the symmetric-group actions restricted to permutations fixing the root. In particular, the two
generating objects in arity 2 are 𝑃 and (12)∗ 𝑃 , corresponding to the two rooted planar corollas obtained
from the two classes in Σ+  2
                              ∕𝐴+
                                2
                                   after choosing the boundary labelled 0 as the root.

Lemma 3.14. There is an isomorphism of operads
                                          𝐼 ∶ 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡 ⟶ 𝑢∗ tr ≤0 𝐒
defined on generators by 𝐼(𝜇) = 𝑃 , 𝐼(𝜏) = 𝐭, 𝐼(𝛽) = 𝐛, and 𝐼(𝛼) = 𝐚.

Proof. By Theorem 3.13, the assignment extends to a map of operads if it satisfies (T), (H1), (H2),
and (P).
We first check the twist–braiding relation (T). Under the assignment in the statement, this becomes
                                             (         )
(3.1)                            𝐭◦1 id𝑃 = id𝑃 ◦(𝐭, 𝐭) ⋅ (12)∗ 𝐛 ⋅ 𝐛
in Aut 𝑢∗ tr ≤0 𝐒(2) (𝑃 ). We interpret this equation using the equivalence 𝕄𝑃 ≅ 𝑃 ↓ 𝐒(0, 3) from Lemma 3.7.
The left-hand side is the full Dehn twist 𝑇0 about the boundary component labelled 0. The morphisms
𝐛 and (12)∗ 𝐛 correspond to 𝐵1,2 and 𝐵2,1 , while id𝑃 ◦(𝐭, 𝐭) gives the boundary twists 𝑇1 𝑇2 . Thus (3.1)
becomes 𝑇0 = 𝑇1 𝑇2 𝐵2,1 𝐵1,2 . Since the boundary twists commute, this is equivalent to
                                          𝐵2,1 𝐵1,2 = 𝑇0 𝑇1−1 𝑇2−1 ,
which is the twist–braiding 2-cell in 𝕄𝑃 . Hence the assigned morphisms satisfy (T).
We next check the pentagon relation (P). We rewrite it as
                    (𝛼◦1 id𝜇 )−1 ⋅ (𝛼◦3 id𝜇 )−1 ⋅ (id𝜇 ◦2 𝛼) ⋅ (𝛼◦2 id𝜇 ) ⋅ (id𝜇 ◦1 𝛼) = 1
                                         GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                       33


in Aut 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(4) ((𝜇◦1 𝜇)◦1 𝜇).
Let   = (𝑃 ◦1 𝑃 )◦1 𝑃 . After replacing the generators by their assigned images, the equivalence 𝕄𝑆       ≅
  ↓ 𝐒(0, 5) of Lemma 3.7 identifies the resulting composite with the loop

                                       (𝐴𝑏,𝑒 )−1 (𝐴𝑎,𝑑 )−1 𝐴𝑐,𝑒 𝐴𝑏,𝑑 𝐴𝑎,𝑐 .
The curves defining these 𝐴-moves are shown in Figure 16. This loop is the boundary of the pentagon
2-cell, and hence represents the identity morphism in 𝕄𝑆 . Its image is therefore the identity in
Aut 𝑢∗ tr ≤0 𝐒(4) ( ), so the assigned morphisms satisfy (P).
The hexagon relations (H1) and (H2) are verified in the same way. They may be rewritten as loops
based at 𝜇◦1 𝜇 and 𝜇◦2 𝜇 in 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(3), respectively. Replacing the generators by their assigned images
and using the corresponding equivalences with the fundamental groupoids of the marking complexes
gives the two hexagon loops shown in Figure 17. Each bounds a hexagon 2-cell, so both composites
are identities. It follows from Theorem 3.13 that the assignment extends to a map of operads 𝐼 ∶
𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡 → 𝑢∗ tr ≤0 𝐒.
It remains to show that 𝐼 is an isomorphism. On objects, 𝐼 is the identity under the identification
of labelled rooted trees. For every 𝑇 ∈ 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡(𝑛), the induced map on automorphism groups is the
identification 𝖯𝖱𝖡𝑛 ≅ Γ0,𝑛+1 of Proposition 3.9. Since these groupoids are connected, each 𝐼(𝑛) is
fully faithful. It is also bijective on objects, and hence is an isomorphism of groupoids. Therefore 𝐼
is an isomorphism of operads.                                                                       □


The operad 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡 admits a cyclic structure in the sense of Definition 2.19. See [16, 50, 55]. We write
𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡cyc for the resulting cyclic operad.
It is enough to describe the action of the transposition (01), since this action, together with the ordinary
symmetric-group actions, determines the full extended symmetric-group action. On objects, (01)
changes the choice of root, making the leg labelled 1 the new root. In particular, (01)∗ 𝜇 = (12)∗ 𝜇.
Together with the cyclic operad axioms, the action on the generating morphisms determines the action
on all morphisms. With our conventions, it is given by
(⋆)            (01)∗ 𝜏 = 𝜏,       (01)∗ 𝛽 = (id𝜇 ◦2 𝜏 −1 ) ⋅ 𝛽 −1   and       (01)∗ 𝛼 = (132)∗ 𝛼 −1 .

Remark 3.15. In [16, Section 3.1], the subscripts in 𝐴−1 231
                                                             record the relabelling of the associator. We
instead write the permutation acting on it, using cycle notation and right actions throughout the paper.
Thus their 𝐴−1
             231
                 is our (132)∗ 𝛼 −1 .

We now show that the operad isomorphism of Lemma 3.14 respects this root-changing action and
therefore gives an isomorphism of cyclic operads.

Proposition 3.16. The isomorphism of operads 𝐼 ∶ 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡 → 𝑢∗ tr ≤0 𝐒 is compatible with the cyclic
structure on 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡. Hence it induces an isomorphism of cyclic operads
                                              𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡cyc ≅ tr ≤0 𝐒.

Proof. Since the (01)-action determines the cyclic structure together with the ordinary symmetric-
group actions, it suffices to verify 𝐼((01)∗ 𝑥) = (01)∗ 𝐼(𝑥) for 𝑥 = 𝜇, 𝜏, 𝛽, 𝛼.
For the generating object, recall that 𝐼(𝜇) = 𝑃 , where 𝑃 is the rooted trivalent corolla with boundary
labels {0, 1, 2}. The objects of tr ≤0 𝐒 are generated by the two rooted planar corollas represented by
𝑃 = id ⋅𝐴+ 2
              and (12)∗ 𝑃 = (12) ⋅ 𝐴+ 2
                                        , where 𝐴+2
                                                    = ⟨(012)⟩. Since (01) and (12) represent the same
nontrivial coset modulo 𝐴2 , we have (01)∗ 𝑃 = (12)∗ 𝑃 . Thus 𝐼((01)∗ 𝜇) = (01)∗ 𝐼(𝜇).
                           +

For the twist generator, 𝐼(𝜏) = 𝐭. The transposition (01) acts trivially on 𝐒(0, 2) by definition, so
𝐼((01)∗ 𝜏) = 𝐼(𝜏) = 𝐭 = (01)∗ 𝐼(𝜏).

the equivalence 𝕄(12)∗ 𝑃 ≅ (12)∗ 𝑃 ↓ tr ≤0 𝐒(3), this morphism is identified with the opposite 𝐵-
move followed by the boundary-twist correction that puts the relabelled marking in standard form.

It remains to check the associativity generator. The labelled rooted graphs in Figure 10 give
                 (01)∗ (𝑃 ◦1 𝑃 ) = (132)∗ (𝑃 ◦2 𝑃 ),       (01)∗ (𝑃 ◦2 𝑃 ) = (132)∗ (𝑃 ◦1 𝑃 ).

Remark 3.17. In the proof of Lemma 3.14, we used diagrammatic depictions of genus-zero mapping
classes and their corresponding paths in the complexes of markings. This is justified by Lemma 3.7
and the discussion in Section 2.6.2, which shows that these representatives are well defined. Figure 10
depicts the action of (01) on the 𝐵- and 𝐴-moves.

3.3. A presentation for maps out of 𝐒. We use ⋅ for categorical composition in the groupoids 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛+
1), write ◦𝑗𝑖 for cyclic operadic composition, and write 𝜉𝑖𝑗 for contractions. When only the underlying
operad structure is involved, we write ◦0𝑖 = ◦𝑖 .
The goal of this subsection is to prove the main presentation theorem for maps out of the modular
operad of surfaces 𝐒. As in the genus-zero case, the proof uses the comparison with complexes of
markings over pants decompositions established in Lemma 3.7. These complexes are connected and
simply connected by Proposition 2.38, so their edges describe generators for the morphisms of 𝐒,
while their 2-cells supply the relations.

Theorem 3.18. Let  be a modular operad in groupoids. A map of modular operads 𝑓 ∶ 𝐒 ⟶  is

Relations G1a, G1b and G2 are added to include the higher genus structure. For instance, equation G2
can be pictured diagrammatically as in Figure 11.
36                              L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON




                                F IGURE 11. Diagram of relation (G2)

Proof. A map of modular operads 𝑓 ∶ 𝐒 →  necessarily determines the images of the generating
morphisms:
                     𝑓 (𝑃 ) = 𝐦, 𝑓 (𝜏) = 𝐭, 𝑓 (𝛽) = 𝐛, 𝑓 (𝛼) = 𝐚, 𝑓 (𝜎) = 𝐬.
Moreover, such a map consists of functors 𝑓 (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) ∶ 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) ⟶ (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) compatible
with the symmetric group actions, operadic compositions, and contractions. Under the equivalence
of Lemma 3.7, the boundaries of the relevant 2-cells in the marking complexes (𝑆𝐺 ) are identified
with the relations (S), (T), (P), (H1), (H2), (G1a), (G1b), and (G2). Therefore any map 𝑓 ∶ 𝐒 → 
gives a tuple (𝐦, 𝐭, 𝐛, 𝐚, 𝐬) satisfying the stated relations.

We now prove the converse. Suppose we are given 𝐦, 𝐭, 𝐛, 𝐚, 𝐬 in  satisfying the stated relations.
Since ob(𝐒) is the free modular operad in sets generated by the genus-zero corolla 𝑃 and (12)∗ 𝑃 , the
assignment ob(𝑓 )(𝑃 ) = 𝐦 ∈ ob((0, 3)) extends uniquely to a map of modular operads in sets
                                      ob(𝑓 ) ∶ ob(𝐒) ⟶ ob().
In particular, the object assignment is compatible with the extended symmetric group actions, operadic
compositions, and contractions. Note that the object 𝑃 ∈ 𝐒(0, 3) is represented by the coset id ⋅𝐴+ 2
                                                                                                      ,
where 𝐴+ 2
           =   ⟨(012)⟩. Thus  (012)∗ 𝑃 = 𝑃 , and the condition

                                            (012)∗ 𝐦 = 𝐦
is precisely the condition needed for the assignment 𝑃 ↦ 𝐦 to respect the stabiliser of the generating
object.

Using the cyclic operad isomorphism 𝖯𝖺𝖱𝖡cyc ≅ tr ≤0 𝐒 from Proposition 3.16, together with Theo-
rem 3.13, the assignments
                        𝜇 ↦ 𝐦,        𝜏 ↦ 𝐭,      𝛽 ↦ 𝐛,       and      𝛼↦𝐚
satisfying (T), (P), (H1), and (H2) define the genus-zero part of 𝑓 , namely a map of cyclic operads
tr ≤0 𝑓 ∶ tr ≤0 𝐒 ⟶ tr ≤0 .

It remains to extend the genus-zero map to all of 𝐒. By Proposition 3.8, every morphism in 𝐒 is obtained
from the generating morphisms 𝛼, 𝛽, 𝜏, 𝜎 by modular operad operations and categorical composition.
                                       GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                   37


Thus, for a morphism
                                        𝜙 ∈ Hom𝐒(𝑔,𝑛+1) (𝐺, 𝐺′ ),
we choose an expression for 𝜙 in terms of these generators and define its image in (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) by
replacing 𝛼, 𝛽, 𝜏, and 𝜎 by 𝐚, 𝐛, 𝐭 and 𝐬.
We must check that this description of 𝑓 (𝜙) is independent of the chosen expression. So suppose that
𝜙 has two expressions
                                     𝜙 = 𝜙1𝑘 ⋯ 𝜙11 = 𝜙2𝑚 ⋯ 𝜙21 ,
in 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1), where each 𝜙𝑟𝑖 is one of the generating morphisms of 𝐒. Then
                          (𝜙2𝑚 ⋯ 𝜙21 )−1 (𝜙1𝑘 ⋯ 𝜙11 ) = id𝐺 ∈ Aut 𝐒(𝑔,𝑛+1) (𝐺).

Under the equivalence of Lemma 3.7, this relation is represented by a loop in the marking complex
(𝑆𝐺 ). Since (𝑆𝐺 ) is connected and simply connected [4, Theorem 5.1], this loop is generated
by the boundaries of the 2-cells of (𝑆𝐺 ). The boundary relations of these 2-cells are exactly the
relations imposed above, together with the formal relations already forced by the modular operad
axioms.
More precisely, the twist–braiding, pentagon, and two hexagon cells are encoded by (T), (P), (H1), and
(H2). The self-duality of the associativity move follows from Mac Lane coherence, and therefore from
the pentagon and hexagon relations already imposed. The genus-one cells in types (1, 1) and (1, 2)
are encoded by (G1a), (G1b), and (G2). The remaining cells express formal compatibility relations
for disjointly supported moves and for the modular operad operations. These hold in  because  is
a modular operad.
It follows that
                           (𝑓 (𝜙2𝑚 ) ⋯ 𝑓 (𝜙21 ))−1 (𝑓 (𝜙1𝑘 ) ⋯ 𝑓 (𝜙11 )) = id𝑓 (𝐺) .
Thus the two expressions for 𝜙 have the same image in , so the assignment on morphisms is well
defined. By construction it is compatible with categorical composition, symmetric group actions,
operadic compositions, and contractions. Hence it defines a map of modular operads 𝑓 ∶ 𝐒 ⟶ .
The theorem follows.
                                                                                                    □

                            4. T HE OPERADIC TWO -LEVEL PRINCIPLE
Grothendieck’s two-level principle predicts that Galois actions on the Teichmüller tower should be
determined by their effect in the first two levels, namely on
                                   ̂0,4 ,
                                   Γ        ̂0,5 ,
                                            Γ        ̂1,1 ,
                                                     Γ        and Γ
                                                                  ̂1,2 .
In this section we formulate and prove an operadic version of this principle. Our main goal is to show
that there exists an action of Gal(ℚ∕ℚ) on the profinite completion of the surface modular operad
𝐒 (Theorem 4.9). Moreover, this action is completely determined by the genus one truncation of ̂
̂                                                                                                    𝐒
(Corollary 4.4).
We begin with a brief discussion of profinite completion for modular operads in groupoids.

4.1. Profinite completion of modular operads in groupoids. We aim to study automorphisms of the
profinite completion ̂
                     𝐒. We begin by explaining how profinite completion extends from groupoids to
modular operads in groupoids. Proposition 2.6 shows that, under the finite-object hypothesis satisfied
by 𝐒, the modular-operad structure can be completed entrywise.

4.1.1. The genus-one truncation. The presentation theorem (Theorem 3.18) shows that maps out of
𝐒 are determined by the images of the generators and relations appearing in genus at most one. We
express this using the truncation formalism introduced in Section 2.5.1 and developed further in Ap-
pendix A.1: tr ≤1  denotes the genus-one truncation of a modular operad , and the truncation functor
admits a left adjoint (tr ≤1 )! .

Corollary 4.3. The counit of the truncation adjunction gives an isomorphism of modular operads in
groupoids
                                        (tr ≤1 )! (tr ≤1 𝐒) ≅ 𝐒.

Proof. By Theorem 3.18, a map out of 𝐒 is uniquely determined by the images of the generators
appearing in tr ≤1 𝐒, and every choice of such images satisfying the stated relations extends uniquely
to a map out of 𝐒. Thus, for every modular operad , restriction induces a natural bijection
                             HomMod(𝒢 ) (𝐒, ) ≅ HomMod≤1 (𝒢 ) (tr ≤1 𝐒, tr ≤1 ).
By the adjunction (tr ≤1 )! ⊣ tr ≤1 , the right-hand side is naturally isomorphic to HomMod(𝒢 ) ((tr ≤1 )! (tr ≤1 𝐒), ).
Under these identifications, the natural bijection is induced by precomposition with the counit
                                                (tr ≤1 )! (tr ≤1 𝐒) ⟶ 𝐒.
Therefore, by Yoneda, the counit is an isomorphism.                                                            □

                                     5. M ODULAR ∞-OPERADS
To take profinite completions of modular operads in spaces, we need a model for homotopy-coherent
modular operads. The relevant indexing category is the modular dendroidal category ℳ, whose ob-
jects are connected genus-graded labelled graphs with non-empty boundary. The modular dendroidal
nerve of a strict modular operad satisfies
                                                 ∏
                                        (N)𝐺 ≅      (N)𝐶𝑣 .
                                                   𝑣∈𝑉𝐺

See [33, Theorem 3.6]. A modular ∞-operad is obtained by replacing this strict product condition by
a Segal condition.
We first recall the modular dendroidal category. Let 𝐺 = (𝐺, 𝜆, 𝓁, 𝜖) be a genus-graded labelled graph.
For each vertex 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉𝐺 , let 𝐻𝑣 be a genus-graded labelled graph whose boundary is identified with
the half-edges incident to 𝑣. We require 𝐻𝑣 to have total genus 𝜖(𝑣), equivalently
                                                      ∑
                                   𝜖(𝑣) = 𝛽1 (𝐻𝑣 ) +       𝜖𝐻𝑣 (𝑤),
                                                      𝑤∈𝑉𝐻𝑣

where 𝜖𝐻𝑣 denotes the genus function on 𝐻𝑣 . Then we can define a new graph 𝐺{𝐻𝑣 }𝑣∈𝑉𝐺 by the
coequaliser
                           ∐         𝑢   ∐         𝜋
                               ↕              𝐻𝑣       𝐺{𝐻𝑣 }𝑣∈𝑉𝐺 .
                            𝑒∈𝑖𝐸𝐺      𝑑    𝑣∈𝑉𝐺

Here, the maps 𝑢 and 𝑑 record the two incidences of each internal edge of 𝐺. The coequaliser glues
the corresponding boundary legs of the graphs 𝐻𝑣 . This is the graph-substitution construction of [32,
Construction 1.18].

Definition 5.1. The modular dendroidal category ℳ is the category whose objects are connected
genus-graded labelled graphs with non-empty boundary. Its morphisms, called graphical maps, are
generated by isomorphisms, the graph-substitution maps 𝐺 → 𝐺{𝐻𝑣 }𝑣∈𝑉𝐺 , and the maps 𝐻𝑣 →
𝐺{𝐻𝑤 }𝑤∈𝑉𝐺 supplied by the coequaliser. See [32, Definition 1.31] for full details.

The category of modular dendroidal objects in ℰ , denoted dMod(ℰ ), is the category of contravariant
functors 𝑋 ∶ ℳ op ⟶ ℰ and natural transformations. We write 𝑋𝐺 for the value of 𝑋 ∈ dMod(ℰ )
on a graph 𝐺 ∈ ℳ, and we write
                                    ℳ[𝐺] ∶= Homℳ (−, 𝐺)
for the representable functor at 𝐺.

Definition 5.2. [32, Section 3.1] Let 𝐺 be a graph with at least one vertex. The Segal core of 𝐺 is the
coequaliser in dMod(Set)
                            ∐                   ∐
                                 ℳ[↕]               ℳ[𝐶𝑣 ]           Sc[𝐺].
                           𝑒∈𝑖𝐸𝐺               𝑣∈𝑉𝐺

The two arrows record the two incidences of each internal edge with the corollas at its endpoints. The
Segal core is a subobject of the representable ℳ[𝐺], with inclusion Sc[𝐺] ⟶ ℳ[𝐺] induced by the
inclusions of the local vertex data ℳ[𝐶𝑣 ] ⟶ ℳ[𝐺].
                                           GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                47


                  𝐻𝑣




                   𝑣
                                            𝐻𝑤


𝐻𝑢                       𝑤



                   𝑢




                                 F IGURE 15. An example of a graphical map

The Segal core detects whether the value of a modular dendroidal object on a graph is determined, up
to homotopy, by its values on the corollas at the vertices. When ℰ is a cofibrantly generated Cartesian
monoidal model category, the category dMod(ℰ ) admits the projective model structure, in which a
map 𝑋 → 𝑌 is a weak equivalence, respectively a fibration, if and only if 𝑋𝐺 → 𝑌𝐺 is a weak
equivalence, respectively a fibration, in ℰ for every 𝐺 ∈ ℳ. See [38, Theorem 11.6.1].
A modular dendroidal object 𝑋 is called Segal if, for every 𝐺 ∈ ℳ, the map induced by the Segal
core inclusion
                             ℝ Map(ℳ[𝐺], 𝑋) ⟶ ℝ Map(Sc[𝐺], 𝑋)
is a weak equivalence of spaces.5 A modular dendroidal object 𝑋 is called reduced if 𝑋↕ =∗.

Definition 5.3. A modular ∞-operad in ℰ is a modular dendroidal object 𝑋 ∶ ℳ op → ℰ satisfying:
      ∙ 𝑋↕ =∗;
      ∙ for every graph 𝐺 ∈ ℳ, the Segal map
                                   ℝ Map(ℳ[𝐺], 𝑋) ⟶ ℝ Map(Sc[𝐺], 𝑋)
         is a weak equivalence of spaces.

We write Mod∞ (ℰ ) for the ∞-category of modular ∞-operads in ℰ . The model-categorical presen-
tation of this ∞-category is recalled in Appendix B.
The condition 𝑋↕ =∗ means that we are considering one-coloured modular ∞-operads. It also sim-
plifies the Segal condition. Indeed, for a reduced object 𝑋, the edge-compatibility conditions in the
Segal core are trivial, since each internal edge contributes a copy of 𝑋↕ =∗. Thus, after replacing 𝑋
by an entrywise fibrant object if necessary, the Segal map may be identified with the comparison
                                                   ∏
                                          𝑋𝐺 ⟶         (𝑋𝐶𝑣 )𝑓 .
                                                         𝑣∈𝑉𝐺

Here (𝑋𝐶𝑣 )𝑓 denotes a fibrant replacement in ℰ , so that the product computes the corresponding ho-
motopy product. Equivalently, a reduced modular dendroidal object is a modular ∞-operad precisely
   5Here ℳ[𝐺] and Sc[𝐺] are regarded as discrete objects of dMod(ℰ ) via the functor Set → ℰ .
48                               L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


when, for every graph 𝐺, the value 𝑋𝐺 is weakly equivalent to the homotopy product of its values on
the corollas at the vertices of 𝐺.
In the cases used in this paper we do not need to worry much about fibrant replacements. If ℰ = 𝒢 ,
every object is fibrant, so the homotopy product is computed by the ordinary product. If ℰ = 𝒮 and
𝑋 is entrywise fibrant, for example if 𝑋 is fibrant in the localised Reedy model structure, the ordinary
product again computes the homotopy product. In this situation the Segal condition can be written
simply as requiring                                   ∏
                                           𝑋𝐺 ⟶           𝑋𝐶𝑣
                                                     𝑣∈𝑉𝐺
to be a weak equivalence.
5.1. The modular dendroidal nerve. Let Mod∞ (ℰ ) denote the ∞-category of modular ∞-operads,
as in Definition B.1. A strict modular operad assigns operations to genus-graded corollas. Viewing
a general genus-graded graph as a pattern for composing such operations, one obtains a modular
dendroidal object by decorating each vertex with an operation of the corresponding genus and arity.
This construction defines the modular dendroidal nerve functor
                                     N ∶ Mod(ℰ ) ⟶ Mod∞ (ℰ ).

Definition 5.4. The nerve of a modular operad  is the contravariant functor N ∶ ℳ op → ℰ defined
on a graph 𝐺 = (𝐺, 𝜆, 𝓁, 𝜖) by
                                             ∏ (                 )
                                 (N)𝐺 ∶=         𝜖(𝑣), |𝑛𝑏(𝑣)| .
                                              𝑣∈𝑉𝐺

In particular, if 𝐶𝑣 is the corolla at a vertex 𝑣, then
                                                      (          )
                                         (N)𝐶𝑣 =  𝜖(𝑣), |𝑛𝑏(𝑣)| .

For a strict modular operad , the Segal map is an isomorphism rather than just a weak equivalence.
Indeed, for every graph 𝐺, the definition of the nerve identifies
                                                   ∏
                                        (N)𝐺 ≅        (N)𝐶𝑣 .
                                                   𝑣∈𝑉𝐺

Thus the nerve of a strict modular operad satisfies the Segal condition strictly. In particular, when
ordinary finite products compute finite homotopy products in ℰ , the object N is a modular ∞-operad.
This applies in the cases used in this paper, for example when ℰ = 𝒢 , and for entrywise fibrant objects
in 𝒮 . In Appendix B, we prove that the modular dendroidal nerve is homotopically fully faithful; see
Theorem B.2.
5.2. Truncations of modular dendroidal objects. We now introduce genus truncations of modular
dendroidal objects. Let ℳ≤𝑔 ⊆ ℳ denote the full subcategory spanned by genus-graded graphs of
total genus at most 𝑔.

Lemma 5.5. The full subcategory ℳ≤𝑔 is a sieve on ℳ. That is, if 𝜙 ∶ 𝐺 → 𝐻 is a morphism in ℳ
and 𝐻 ∈ ℳ≤𝑔 , then 𝐺 ∈ ℳ≤𝑔 .

Proof. If 𝐺 is the exceptional edge, the assertion is immediate. Otherwise, the active–embedding
factorisation of [32, Proposition 2.14] expresses 𝜙 as
                                      𝐺 ⟶ 𝐺{𝐾𝑣 }𝑣∈𝑉𝐺 ⟶ 𝐻,
where the first map is given by graph substitution and the second is a graphical embedding. Each 𝐾𝑣
has total genus 𝜖𝐺 (𝑣), so graph substitution preserves total genus. Moreover, a graphical embedding
preserves vertex genera and cannot decrease the first Betti number. Consequently,
                                             (            )
                                   𝑔(𝐺) = 𝑔 𝐺{𝐾𝑣 }𝑣∈𝑉𝐺 ≤ 𝑔(𝐻).
Thus 𝐻 ∈ ℳ≤𝑔 implies 𝐺 ∈ ℳ≤𝑔 .                                                                       □
                                          GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                         49

                                     op
We write dMod≤𝑔 (ℰ ) = Fun(ℳ≤𝑔 , ℰ ) for the category of genus-𝑔 truncated modular dendroidal
objects in ℰ . The inclusion 𝑖𝑔 ∶ ℳ≤𝑔 ↪ ℳ induces a restriction functor
                                     𝑖∗𝑔 ∶ dMod(ℰ ) ⟶ dMod≤𝑔 (ℰ ).
We call 𝑖∗𝑔 𝑋 the genus-𝑔 truncation of 𝑋 and write it as tr ≤𝑔 𝑋.

                       6. F ROM PROFINITE GROUPOIDS TO PROFINITE SPACES
Theorem 4.9 constructs an action of the monoid ̂     on the modular operad ̂
                                                                            𝐒 in profinite groupoids.
In this section, we pass from this strict action to homotopy automorphisms and construct a faithful
-action on the associated modular ∞-operad BN
̂                                               ̂ ̂ 𝐒 in profinite spaces.
We also show that BN𝐒
                   ̂ is a modular ∞-operad whose corolla values model the étale homotopy types
of the tangent-vector moduli stacks. Comparing these two constructions identifies their genus-one
truncations and gives the operadic two-level principle.

6.1. Profinite completion of modular ∞-operads. We next explain why profinite completion of
modular dendroidal objects may be computed entrywise. Let 𝒦 be a small category with finite hom-
sets and let 𝐂 be a small category with finite limits. A functor 𝐴 ∶ 𝒦 op → 𝐂 is finitely coskeletal if it is
the right Kan extension of its restriction to a finite full subcategory of 𝒦 op . We write Funf cosk (𝒦 op , 𝐂)
for the full subcategory of such functors. The dual of [12, Theorem 2.3] gives an equivalence
                                  (                     )
                              Pro Funfcosk (𝒦 op , 𝐂) ≃ Fun(𝒦 op , Pro(𝐂)).

The modular dendroidal category ℳ is small and has finite hom-sets, and hence is the filtered union
of its finite full subcategories. For each finite full subcategory 𝒟 ⊆ ℳ, restriction to 𝒟 op has a right
adjoint cosk 𝒟 . Every
                    (     modular
                           )       dendroidal object 𝑋 is canonically the limit of the finitely coskeletal
diagrams cosk 𝒟 𝑋|𝒟 op , as 𝒟 ranges over the finite full subcategories of ℳ. Indeed, after evalu-
ation at a graph 𝐺, this system is constant with value 𝑋𝐺 once 𝒟 contains 𝐺. It follows that pro-𝐂
completion of modular dendroidal objects is computed entrywise.
We apply this with ℰ = 𝒮 or 𝒢 . In the groupoid case, 𝐂 is the category of finite groupoids. In the
spatial case, 𝐂 is the category of lean simplicial sets. These are degreewise finite simplicial sets that are
𝑛-coskeletal for some 𝑛 ≥ 0, as in [12, Section 2]. In this case, the resulting model structure on Pro(𝐂)
agrees with Quick’s model structure on profinite spaces by [12, Example 5.5 and Corollary 6.6].

Definition 6.1. Let 𝑋 ∶ ℳ op → ℰ be a modular dendroidal object. Its profinite completion is the
modular dendroidal object
                                 ̂ ∶ ℳ op ⟶ Pro(𝐂),
                                 𝑋                               ̂ 𝐺=𝑋

obtained by applying pro-𝐂 completion entrywise.

Profinite completion does not in general preserve finite products. Consequently, even if 𝑋 satisfies
the Segal condition, its entrywise profinite completion 𝑋̂ need not do so. For groupoids, however,
Proposition 2.6 shows that completion preserves finite products under the finiteness hypothesis used
in Section 4.1. The following lemma applies this comparison to the Segal condition.
We equip 𝒢̂ with the model structure whose weak equivalences are those of [39, Definition 4.8].
We write Mod∞ (𝒢̂) for the ∞-category presented by the relative category of modular ∞-operads
𝑋 ∶ ℳ op → 𝒢̂. A morphism 𝑓 ∶ 𝑋 → 𝑌 is a weak equivalence in this relative category if
𝑓𝐺 ∶ 𝑋𝐺 → 𝑌𝐺 is a weak equivalence in 𝒢̂ for every 𝐺 ∈ ℳ.

Lemma 6.2. Let 𝑋 ∶ ℳ op → 𝒢 be a modular ∞-operad such that 𝑋𝐶 has finitely many objects for
every corolla 𝐶 ∈ ℳ. Then entrywise profinite completion defines an object
                                               ̂ ∈ Mod∞ (𝒢̂).
                                               𝑋

If  is an entrywise fibrant modular operad in profinite groupoids, its nerve satisfies the Segal condition

For a modular operad in groupoids satisfying the finiteness hypothesis of Definition 4.1, the following

Lemma 6.3. Let  be a modular operad in groupoids such that (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) has finitely many objects

Proposition 6.7. The morphism 𝑞 ∶ 𝐒cof ⟶ 𝐒 is an entrywise trivial fibration. Consequently, it is a
cofibrant replacement of 𝐒 in Mod(𝒢 ).

Proof. For every 𝑔, 𝑛 ≥ 0, the functor 𝑞(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) induces the identity under the defining identification
of morphism sets, and is therefore fully faithful.

unit, an object of 𝐒(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) is represented by a connected trivalent graph with its vertex and boundary

to itself.
Thus 𝑞(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) is fully faithful and surjective on objects, and hence is a trivial fibration of groupoids.
Since fibrations and weak equivalences in Mod(𝒢 ) are defined entrywise (Theorem A.5), 𝑞 is a trivial
fibration of modular operads. Its source is cofibrant by Lemma 6.6, so 𝑞 is a cofibrant replacement of
𝐒.                                                                                                        □

6.2.2. Homotopy automorphisms. We use an elementary notion of homotopy for modular operads
in groupoids and profinite groupoids. Let ℐ be the finite groupoid with objects {0, 1} and a unique
morphism between any two objects, regarded as a profinite groupoid when necessary. For a modular
operad  in groupoids or profinite groupoids, let ℐ denote the modular operad defined entrywise by
                                      (ℐ )(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) = (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)ℐ .
The modular-operad operations on ℐ are defined pointwise. Evaluation at the two objects of ℐ
gives morphisms 𝑑0 , 𝑑1 ∶ ℐ → .
Given morphisms 𝑓 , 𝑔 ∶  → , a homotopy from 𝑓 to 𝑔 is a morphism of modular operads 𝐻 ∶
 → ℐ satisfying 𝑑0 𝐻 = 𝑓 and 𝑑1 𝐻 = 𝑔. Equivalently, a homotopy is a natural isomorphism from
𝑓 to 𝑔 compatible with the modular-operad structure, continuous in the profinite case.
This defines an equivalence relation on morphisms and is compatible with composition. We write
[, ] for the resulting set of homotopy classes. In particular, [, ] is a monoid, and we write
HoAut() for its group of units. We write Aut 0 () for the group of strict automorphisms of  that
fix every object.

Proposition 6.9. The map
                                           Aut 0 (̂
                                                  𝐒) → HoAut(̂
                                                             𝐒)
is injective.

𝐹 fixes objects, it induces continuous bijections on morphism sets, which are homeomorphisms of
profinite sets. Hence 𝐹 is a strict automorphism, proving surjectivity.
Finally, if two object-fixing automorphisms determine the same component, (6.10) implies that they
are homotopic. They are equal by Proposition 6.9, proving injectivity.                          □

6.4. Étale homotopy types and the profinite-space model. We now take classifying spaces before
profinite completion and show that BN𝐒 ̂ defines a modular ∞-operad in profinite spaces. Its corolla
values will be identified with the étale homotopy types of the tangent-vector moduli stacks. To verify
the Segal condition, we must show that profinite completion preserves the finite products occurring in
its Segal maps.
We write 𝚂 for the ∞-category of spaces and 𝚂𝜋 ⊂ 𝚂 for the full subcategory of 𝜋-finite spaces. The
∞-category of profinite spaces is ̂
                                  𝚂 ∶= Pro(𝚂𝜋 ). Profinite completion (−)
                                                                       ̂ ∶ Pro(𝚂) → ̂𝚂 is left adjoint
to the inclusion. We regard spaces as constant prospaces when applying this functor.
Let 𝚂<∞ ⊂ 𝚂 denote the full subcategory of truncated spaces, and write 𝜏<∞ ∶ Pro(𝚂) → Pro(𝚂<∞ )
for protruncation. Following [34, Notation 2.11], let  ⊂ Pro(𝚂<∞ ) be the smallest full subcategory
containing ̂
           𝚂 and closed under geometric realisations, retracts, and cofiltered limits. We call a prospace
𝑋 admissible if 𝜏<∞ 𝑋 lies in .

Proposition 6.18. Let 𝑔1 , … , 𝑔𝑟 ≥ 0 and 𝑛1 , … , 𝑛𝑟 ≥ 0. Then the natural map

Proof. Since 𝑛𝑖 + 1 > 0, each group Γ𝑔𝑖 ,𝑛𝑖 +1 is torsion-free and admits a cocompact classifying space

a finite CW model for BΓ𝑔𝑖 ,𝑛𝑖 +1 .
Consequently, each BΓ𝑔𝑖 ,𝑛𝑖 +1 is a compact object of 𝚂. By [34, Observation 2.12], its protruncation
belongs to , so it is admissible. Proposition 6.15 now gives the required equivalence.            □
