                     A PPENDIX A. H OMOTOPY T HEORY OF M ODULAR O PERADS
Modular operads can be viewed as algebras over a certain coloured operad (cf. [20, Appendix A]).
This allows us to obtain model structures on categories of modular operads from general results con-
cerning algebras over coloured operads. Since our arguments rely on several specific features of the
coloured operad governing modular operads, we begin with a brief review of coloured operads and
their algebras.

Definition A.1. Let ℭ be a nonempty set of colours. A ℭ-coloured sequence in ℰ is a collection of
objects
                                        (𝑐1 , … , 𝑐𝑘 ; 𝑐0 )
indexed by lists (𝑐1 , … , 𝑐𝑘 ; 𝑐0 ) of colours in ℭ, together with right actions of the symmetric groups.
Thus, for each 𝜎 ∈ Σ𝑘 , there is an isomorphism
                                                        𝜎∗
                                                             ← (𝑐𝜎(1) , … , 𝑐𝜎(𝑘) ; 𝑐0 ).
                                 (𝑐1 , … , 𝑐𝑘 ; 𝑐0 ) ←←←←←←←→

A ℭ-coloured operad is a ℭ-coloured sequence  equipped with:
     (1) unit maps 𝜄𝑐 ∶ 1ℰ → (𝑐; 𝑐) for each 𝑐 ∈ ℭ;
     (2) composition maps
                                                        ◦𝑖
         (𝑐1 , … , 𝑐𝑘 ; 𝑐0 ) ⊗ (𝑑1 , … , 𝑑𝑗 ; 𝑑0 ) ←←←←←←→
                                                           ← (𝑐1 , … , 𝑐𝑖−1 , 𝑑1 , … , 𝑑𝑗 , 𝑐𝑖+1 , … , 𝑐𝑘 ; 𝑐0 ),
         defined whenever 𝑐𝑖 = 𝑑0 and 1 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑘.
These data are required to satisfy the standard associativity, unitality, and equivariance axioms.
                                             GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                            63


A map 𝑓 ∶  →  of coloured operads is a map of the underlying coloured sequences that respects
the units, compositions, and symmetric group actions. The category of ℭ-coloured operads in ℰ is
denoted Opℭ (ℰ ). For further details, see [10, Definition 1.1] or [19, §2.2].

Definition A.2. Let  be a ℭ-coloured operad. A -algebra in ℰ is a collection of objects {𝑋(𝑐)}𝑐∈ℭ
together with structure maps
                              (𝑐1 , … , 𝑐𝑘 ; 𝑐0 ) ⊗ 𝑋(𝑐1 ) ⊗ ⋯ ⊗ 𝑋(𝑐𝑘 ) ⟶ 𝑋(𝑐0 )
satisfying associativity, unitality, and equivariance with respect to the operad structure. The category
of such algebras is denoted Alg (ℰ ).

Example A.3. The operad of modular operads is an ℕ × ℕ≥0 -coloured operad . We use the
convention that the colour (𝑔, 𝑟) corresponds to the (𝑔, 𝑟 + 1)-component of a modular operad. The set
                                         (                                )
                                     (𝑔1 , 𝑟1 ), … , (𝑔𝑛 , 𝑟𝑛 ); (𝑔, 𝑟)
consists of strict isomorphism classes of connected genus-graded, labelled graphs 𝐺 = (𝐺, 𝜆, 𝓁, 𝜖)
with 𝑛 vertices and 𝑟 + 1 legs, satisfying:
       ∙ the vertex labelled by 𝑖 has valence |𝑛𝑏(𝑣𝜆(𝑖) )| = 𝑟𝑖 + 1 and genus 𝜖(𝑣𝜆(𝑖) ) = 𝑔𝑖 ;
                                         ∑
       ∙ the total genus is 𝑔 = 𝛽1 (𝐺) + 𝑛𝑖=1 𝑔𝑖 .
For 𝜎 ∈ Σ𝑛 , permutation of the vertex labelling induces
                 (                                )     (                                             )
       𝜎 ∗ ∶  (𝑔1 , 𝑟1 ), … , (𝑔𝑛 , 𝑟𝑛 ); (𝑔, 𝑟) ⟶  (𝑔𝜎(1) , 𝑟𝜎(1) ), … , (𝑔𝜎(𝑛) , 𝑟𝜎(𝑛) ); (𝑔, 𝑟) ,
with the indexing determined by our convention for right symmetric-group actions.
When the ambient category ℰ is understood, we regard this set-valued coloured operad as an operad in
ℰ by taking coproducts of copies of the monoidal unit. For 𝒮 and 𝒢 , this is simply the discrete-object
functor.
Operadic composition is defined by graph substitution. Given
              (                               )              (                                     )
     𝐺 ∈  (𝑔1 , 𝑟1 ), … , (𝑔𝑛 , 𝑟𝑛 ); (𝑔, 𝑟) , 𝐻𝑣𝜆(𝑖) ∈  (ℎ1 , 𝑠1 ), … , (ℎ𝑚 , 𝑠𝑚 ); (𝑔𝑖 , 𝑟𝑖 ) ,
the composite 𝐺◦𝑖 𝐻𝑣𝜆(𝑖) is obtained by substituting 𝐻𝑣𝜆(𝑖) into the vertex 𝑣𝜆(𝑖) of 𝐺:
                                              𝐺◦𝑖 𝐻𝑣𝜆(𝑖) ∶= 𝐺{𝐻𝑣𝜆(𝑖) }.
As in [32, Construction 1.18], this defines an element of
             (                                                                                                )
          (𝑔1 , 𝑟1 ), … , (𝑔𝑖−1 , 𝑟𝑖−1 ), (ℎ1 , 𝑠1 ), … , (ℎ𝑚 , 𝑠𝑚 ), (𝑔𝑖+1 , 𝑟𝑖+1 ), … , (𝑔𝑛 , 𝑟𝑛 ); (𝑔, 𝑟) .

Associativity, equivariance, and unitality follow from the corresponding properties of graph substitu-
tion. For a detailed construction and verification, see [20, Appendix A] or [42, §13.4].

Proposition A.4. The category of algebras over the coloured operad  is naturally isomorphic to
the category of modular operads in ℰ :
                                               Alg (ℰ ) ≅ Mod(ℰ ).

This identification allows us to import general results about algebras over coloured operads. In par-
ticular, under suitable hypotheses on the base category ℰ , it gives a transferred model structure on
Mod(ℰ ).
We will use this when ℰ is one of the following Cartesian monoidal model categories:
       ∙ the category of simplicial sets 𝒮 , equipped with the Kan–Quillen model structure;
       ∙ the category of groupoids 𝒢 , equipped with the canonical model structure in which weak
         equivalences are equivalences of categories [2];
64                                L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


In each case, ℰ is a cofibrantly generated Cartesian monoidal model category with cofibrant unit, a
symmetric monoidal fibrant replacement functor, and a cocommutative coalgebra interval. Therefore
Theorem 2.1 of [10] applies and we have the following.

Theorem A.5. For each of the categories ℰ listed above, the category Mod(ℰ ) admits a cofibrantly
generated model category structure in which a map of modular operads 𝑓 ∶  ⟶  is a weak
equivalence, respectively a fibration, if and only if each component
                                𝑓 (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) ∶ (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) ⟶ (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)
is a weak equivalence, respectively a fibration, in ℰ , for all 𝑔 ≥ 0 and 𝑛 ≥ 0.

In this model structure, the fibrant objects are precisely the modular operads that are entrywise fibrant
in ℰ . In particular, since every groupoid is fibrant in the canonical model structure, every modular
operad in Mod(𝒢 ) is fibrant.
The cofibrant objects are less immediate to characterize. For modular operads in groupoids, we use an
adaptation of a criterion of Horel [39]. Let ob ∶ 𝒢 ⟶ Set denote the functor sending a groupoid to
its set of objects. It has a left adjoint Disc, sending a set to the corresponding discrete groupoid, and
a right adjoint Codisc, sending a set to the corresponding codiscrete groupoid. Since these functors
preserve finite products, they induce functors between modular operads in sets and modular operads
in groupoids, denoted by the same symbols. Thus there are adjunctions
            Disc ∶ Mod(Set) ⇄ Mod(𝒢 ) ∶ ob,            ob ∶ Mod(𝒢 ) ⇄ Mod(Set) ∶ Codisc .
We call a morphism in Mod(Set) entrywise surjective if it is surjective in every component (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1).

Proposition A.6. A morphism 𝑢 ∶  →  in Mod(𝒢 ) is a cofibration if and only if
                                        ob(𝑢) ∶ ob() ⟶ ob()
has the left lifting property with respect to every entrywise surjective morphism in Mod(Set).

Proof. This is the modular-operad analogue of [39, Proposition 6.8].
Suppose first that 𝑢 is a cofibration, and let 𝑣 ∶  →  be entrywise surjective. For every (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1),
the functor
                    Codisc(𝑣(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)) ∶ Codisc((𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)) ⟶ Codisc((𝑔, 𝑛 + 1))
is a trivial fibration of groupoids. Hence Codisc(𝑣) is a trivial fibration in Mod(𝒢 ). By the adjunction
ob ⊣ Codisc, any lifting problem for ob(𝑢) against 𝑣 corresponds to a lifting problem for 𝑢 against
Codisc(𝑣), which admits a solution.
Conversely, suppose that ob(𝑢) has the stated lifting property, and let 𝑝 ∶  →  be a trivial fibration
in Mod(𝒢 ). For every (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1), the functor
                                𝑝(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) ∶ (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1) ⟶ (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1)
is fully faithful and surjective on objects. Since ob(𝑝) is entrywise surjective, the induced lifting
problem in Mod(Set) admits a solution. Thus we obtain a lift on objects, compatible with the modular-
operad structure.
At each (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1), full faithfulness of 𝑝 extends this lift uniquely to a functor on morphisms. For each
modular-operad operation, the two morphisms obtained by applying the operation before and after the
lift have the same source and target and the same image under 𝑝. They are therefore equal. Thus the lift
                                                               𝑗                       𝑗
commutes with the Σ+   𝑛 -actions, the partial compositions ◦𝑖 , and the contractions 𝜉𝑖 . It is consequently
a morphism of modular operads, so 𝑢 is a cofibration.                                                      □

Corollary A.7. A modular operad  ∈ Mod(𝒢 ) is cofibrant whenever ob() has the left lifting
property with respect to every entrywise surjective morphism in Mod(Set).

Proof. Apply Proposition A.6 to the unique morphism from the initial modular operad to .                 □
                                       GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                        65


The functors Π1 ∶ 𝒮 → 𝒢 and B ∶ 𝒢 → 𝒮 preserve finite products and therefore induce an adjunction
                                                    Π1
(A.1)                                  Mod(𝒮 )                  Mod(𝒢 ).
                                                        B

Since the model structures on modular operads are defined entrywise, and B sends isofibrations and
trivial isofibrations of groupoids to Kan fibrations and trivial fibrations of simplicial sets, the adjunc-
tion (A.1) is a Quillen adjunction.

Lemma A.8. The adjunction (A.1) is a Quillen adjunction, and its right derived functor is fully faith-
ful. In particular, for all ,  ∈ Mod(𝒢 ), the induced map
                                                               (        )
                             ℝ MapMod(𝒢 ) (, ) ⟶ ℝ MapMod(𝒮 ) B, B
is a weak equivalence of spaces.

Proof. It remains to prove that the right derived functor of B is fully faithful. Since every groupoid is
fibrant, every object of Mod(𝒢 ) is fibrant. Let  ∈ Mod(𝒢 ), and choose a cofibrant replacement
                                                            ∼
                                            𝑞 ∶ 𝑄B ←←←→
                                                       ← B
in Mod(𝒮 ). The derived counit at  is represented by
                                                 Π1 𝑞              𝜖
                                      Π1 𝑄B ←←←←←←←→            ← .
                                                    ← Π1 B ←←←←←→
Since every simplicial set is cofibrant, applying Π1 entrywise to 𝑞 gives an entrywise equivalence
of groupoids. Moreover, 𝜖 ∶ Π1 B →  is an entrywise equivalence, since Π1 B𝐺 → 𝐺 is an
equivalence for every groupoid 𝐺. Thus the derived counit is a weak equivalence, and the right derived
functor of B is fully faithful.                                                                     □

A.1. Truncation of Modular Operads. We now define genus truncations of modular operads by
restricting the coloured operad  governing modular operads. Recall that our colours are pairs
(ℎ, 𝑝), where ℎ is the genus and 𝑝 + 1 is the number of boundary components.
For 𝑔 ≥ 0, let ≤𝑔 be the coloured suboperad of  whose colours are the pairs (ℎ, 𝑝) with
0 ≤ ℎ ≤ 𝑔 and 𝑝 ≥ 0. Its operations are those operations of  whose output colour has genus at
most 𝑔. Thus
                                      (                               )
                              ≤𝑔 (ℎ1 , 𝑝1 ), … , (ℎ𝑛 , 𝑝𝑛 ); (ℎ, 𝑝)
is the set of strict isomorphism classes of connected genus-graded, labelled graphs 𝐺 = (𝐺, 𝜆, 𝓁, 𝜖)
with 𝑛 vertices and 𝑝 + 1 legs, such that the vertex labelled by 𝑖 has valence |𝑛𝑏(𝑣𝜆(𝑖) )| = 𝑝𝑖 + 1 and
                                                 ∑
genus 𝜖(𝑣𝜆(𝑖) ) = ℎ𝑖 , and such that ℎ = 𝛽1 (𝐺) + 𝑖 ℎ𝑖 ≤ 𝑔.
Graph substitution preserves the total output genus, and hence ≤𝑔 is a coloured suboperad of .
A genus-𝑔 truncated modular operad in ℰ is an algebra over ≤𝑔 . We write
                                      Mod≤𝑔 (ℰ ) ∶= Alg≤𝑔 (ℰ )
for the category of genus-𝑔 truncated modular operads. Concretely, an object of Mod≤𝑔 (ℰ ) consists
of the entries (ℎ, 𝑝 + 1) for 0 ≤ ℎ ≤ 𝑔 and 𝑝 ≥ 0, together with all composition and contraction
operations whose output has genus at most 𝑔. Thus compositions (ℎ1 , 𝑝1 + 1) ⊗ (ℎ2 , 𝑝2 + 1) →
(ℎ1 +ℎ2 , 𝑝1 +𝑝2 ) are retained when ℎ1 +ℎ2 ≤ 𝑔 and 𝑝1 +𝑝2 ≥ 1, while contractions 𝜉𝑖𝑗 ∶ (ℎ, 𝑝+1) →
(ℎ + 1, 𝑝 − 1) are retained for 0 ≤ 𝑖 < 𝑗 ≤ 𝑝, 𝑝 ≥ 2, and ℎ + 1 ≤ 𝑔.
The inclusion of coloured operads
                                          𝜄𝑔 ∶ ≤𝑔 ↪ 
induces a restriction functor
                                 tr ∗≤𝑔 ∶= 𝜄∗𝑔 ∶ Mod(ℰ ) ⟶ Mod≤𝑔 (ℰ ),
66                                 L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


which we call the genus-𝑔 truncation functor. It admits a left adjoint (tr ≤𝑔 )! , given by extension
of scalars, and a right adjoint (tr ≤𝑔 )∗ by the categorical-extension criterion of Drummond–Cole and
Hackney [20, Theorem 3.8]:
                                                      (tr ≤𝑔 )!

                                                      tr ∗≤𝑔
(A.2)                                  Mod(ℰ )                    Mod≤𝑔 (ℰ ).

                                                     (tr ≤𝑔 )∗

The left adjoint generalizes the modular envelope; see [61], [20, Appendix A], and [30, §4] for related
constructions.
The inclusion 𝜄𝑔 is a categorical extension in the sense of [20, Definition 3.2]. Operations whose
input and output colours all have genus at most 𝑔 are unchanged by the inclusion. If the output genus
is at most 𝑔 and one input has genus greater than 𝑔, then the operation space is empty, since ℎ =
         ∑
𝛽1 (𝐺) + 𝑖 ℎ𝑖 with all terms nonnegative. Thus the full-faithfulness and ideal conditions of [20,
Remark 4.16] are satisfied.

Proposition A.9. Let ℰ be a cofibrantly generated Cartesian monoidal model category with cofi-
brant unit, a symmetric monoidal fibrant replacement functor, and a cocommutative coalgebra inter-
val. Then both Mod(ℰ ) and Mod≤𝑔 (ℰ ) admit cofibrantly generated model structures in which weak
equivalences and fibrations are defined entrywise.
Moreover, tr ∗≤𝑔 preserves weak equivalences and fibrations, both adjoints in (A.2) are fully faithful,
and there are canonical isomorphisms
                  tr ∗≤𝑔 (tr ≤𝑔 )! ≅ idMod≤𝑔 (ℰ )      and          tr ∗≤𝑔 (tr ≤𝑔 )∗ ≅ idMod≤𝑔 (ℰ ) .

Proof. The model structures follow from Theorem 2.1 of [10], applied to  and ≤𝑔 . Since
weak equivalences and fibrations are defined entrywise, restriction along 𝜄𝑔 preserves both.
By the preceding discussion and [20, Theorem 3.8], tr ∗≤𝑔 has both adjoints. The genus-additivity
formula shows that no operation involving a colour of genus greater than 𝑔 can contribute to an output
of genus at most 𝑔. Consequently, restricting either algebraic extension back to ≤𝑔 recovers the
original truncated algebra. The displayed isomorphisms follow, and hence both adjoints are fully
faithful.                                                                                           □

As for modular operads, cyclic operads are algebras over a coloured operad. The coloured operad
governing cyclic operads is identified with the genus-zero truncation
                                                     ≅ ≤0 .
Its operations are represented by labelled unrooted trees, equivalently by genus-zero graphs 𝑇 ∈
≤0 ((0, 𝑘1 ), … , (0, 𝑘𝑛 ); (0, 𝑝)). Here (0, 𝑝) corresponds to 𝑝 + 1 boundary components. See [20,
Example A3] or [44, Section 1.6.4].

Proposition A.10. There is a natural isomorphism of categories
                                              Alg (ℰ ) ≅ Cyc(ℰ ).

As a consequence of Proposition A.10, Theorem 2.1 of [10] applies to cyclic operads as well. Thus
Cyc(ℰ ) admits a cofibrantly generated model category structure in which a map of cyclic operads
𝑓 ∶  →  is a weak equivalence, respectively a fibration, if and only if each component 𝑓 (𝑛) ∶
(𝑛) ⟶ (𝑛) is a weak equivalence, respectively a fibration, in ℰ , for all 𝑛 ≥ 0. Moreover, under
the identification Mod≤0 (ℰ ) ≅ Cyc(ℰ ), the genus-zero truncation functor becomes
                                        tr ∗≤0 ∶ Mod(ℰ ) ⟶ Cyc(ℰ ).
                                         GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                           67


It preserves weak equivalences and fibrations, and hence is the right adjoint in the Quillen adjunction
                                   (tr ≤0 )! ∶ Cyc(ℰ ) ⇄ Mod(ℰ ) ∶ tr ∗≤0 .

                      A PPENDIX B. H OMOTOPICAL PROPERTIES OF THE NERVE
Throughout this section, we take ℰ = 𝒮 or 𝒢 and write dMod(ℰ ) = Fun(ℳ op , ℰ ) for the category
of modular dendroidal objects. To study the homotopical properties of the nerve, we will use both
the projective and Reedy model structures on this category. The projective model structure, denoted
dMod(ℰ )𝑝 , has weak equivalences and fibrations defined entrywise; see [38, Theorem 11.6.1].
Since ℳ is a generalised Reedy category [32, Theorem 2.22], dMod(ℰ ) also admits the Reedy model
structure, denoted dMod(ℰ )𝑅 ; see [11, Theorem 1.6]. These two model structures present the same
homotopy theory: by [32, Lemma 3.9], the identity functor induces a Quillen equivalence
(B.1)                                 dMod(ℰ )𝑝             dMod(ℰ )𝑅 .

A modular dendroidal object 𝑋 is reduced if 𝑋↕ =∗. Its reduction is defined by the pushout

                                  𝑋↕ × ℳ[↕]                ℳ[↕]∕ Aut ℳ (↕)


                                       𝑋                          𝑋∗ .

Here the automorphism group acts on the representable by postcomposition. The upper horizontal
map is the projection followed by the quotient map, and the left-hand map is evaluation.
Evaluating at the exceptional edge gives (𝑋∗ )↕ =∗. Every map from 𝑋 to a reduced object factors
uniquely through 𝑋∗ , so the reduction functor (−)∗ ∶ dMod(ℰ ) → dMod(ℰ )∗ is left adjoint to the
inclusion of reduced objects. This category carries the corresponding projective and Reedy model
structures, denoted dMod(ℰ )∗,𝑝 and dMod(ℰ )∗,𝑅 , respectively.
Recall the set of reduced Segal-core inclusions
                                   𝑆 = {Sc[𝐺]∗ ⟶ ℳ[𝐺]∗ ∣ 𝐺 ∈ ℳ}.
We make these maps weak equivalences by taking the left Bousfield localisation of each model struc-
ture at 𝑆. We denote the resulting model structures by 𝑆 dMod(ℰ )∗,𝑝 and 𝑆 dMod(ℰ )∗,𝑅 . In the
projective case, the fibrant objects are precisely the projectively fibrant reduced Segal objects; in the
Reedy case, they are the Reedy fibrant reduced Segal objects.
The identity adjunction (B.1) remains a Quillen equivalence after localisation. Moreover, the two
localisations have the same weak equivalences, allowing us to use either model structure when estab-
lishing local equivalences.

Definition B.1. We write Mod∞ (ℰ ) for the ∞-category presented by 𝑆 dMod(ℰ )∗,𝑝 and call it the
∞-category of modular ∞-operads. Equivalently, Mod∞ (ℰ ) is presented by the relative category
whose objects are reduced Segal objects in dMod(ℰ ) (Definition 5.3), whose morphisms are natural
transformations, and whose weak equivalences are defined entrywise.

Theorem B.2. For any ,  ∈ Mod(ℰ ), the nerve induces a weak equivalence
                           ℝ MapMod(ℰ ) (, ) ⟶ ℝ MapMod∞ (ℰ ) (N, N).

We delay the proof of Theorem B.2 while we establish some intermediate results about the nerve of a
free modular operad.
Let 𝐺 = (𝐺, 𝜆, 𝓁, 𝜖) be a labelled graph with vertices 𝑣𝜆(1) , … , 𝑣𝜆(𝑘) . Write 𝜖(𝑣𝜆(𝑖) ) = 𝑔𝑖 and |𝑛𝑏(𝑣𝜆(𝑖) )| =
𝑛𝑖 + 1. Let 𝑋𝐺 be the genus-graded set with one generator 𝑥𝑖 ∈ 𝑋𝐺 (𝑔𝑖 , 𝑛𝑖 + 1) for each labelled vertex
and no other generators. We write  (𝑋𝐺 ) for the free modular operad (Definition 2.24) on 𝑋𝐺 , where
68                               L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


each 𝑥𝑖 generates a free Σ+
                          𝑛𝑖 -orbit under relabelling of the incident half-edges at the corresponding
vertex.
To compare representables with nerves of free modular operads, we keep track of which generator
labels each vertex of 𝐺. We therefore define the decorated representable
                                    𝑍𝐺 ∶= ℳ[𝐺] × {(𝑥1 , … , 𝑥𝑘 )},
where the second factor is the constant singleton containing the tuple of vertex generators. This records
the choice of 𝑥𝑖 at 𝑣𝜆(𝑖) , which determines the comparison with N (𝑋𝐺 ). We write 𝑍𝐺,∗ for its
reduction.
The relation between 𝑍𝐺,∗ and  (𝑋𝐺 ) is expressed by the adjunction for the modular dendroidal
nerve. Namely, N ∶ Mod(ℰ ) → dMod(ℰ )∗ admits a left adjoint 𝜏 ∶ dMod(ℰ )∗ → Mod(ℰ ) satisfying
𝜏(𝑍𝐺,∗ ) ≅  (𝑋𝐺 ). In particular, 𝜏 sends a reduced corolla representable to the free modular operad
on one generator in the corresponding genus and valence. The counit 𝜏N →  is an isomorphism;
see [33, Theorem 3.6]. We first check that this adjunction is compatible with the projective model
structure.

Proposition B.3. The adjunction
                                                      𝜏
                                     dMod(ℰ )∗,𝑝           Mod(ℰ )
                                                      N

is a Quillen pair.

Proof. Let 𝑓 ∶  →  be a fibration, respectively a trivial fibration, in Mod(ℰ ). For every graph
𝐺 ∈ ℳ, the strict Segal condition gives
                                           ∏ (                 )
                                  (N𝑓 )𝐺 ≅    𝑓 𝜖(𝑣), |𝑛𝑏(𝑣)| .
                                              𝑣∈𝑉𝐺

Each factor is a fibration, respectively a trivial fibration, and finite products preserve both classes of
maps. For the exceptional edge, the empty product gives the identity of the terminal object. Thus N
preserves projective fibrations and trivial fibrations, and is right Quillen.                           □

The decorated representable 𝑍𝐺 records the graph 𝐺 with its specified vertex generators. To describe
the whole nerve N (𝑋𝐺 ), we also consider graphs whose vertices are decorated by these genera-
tors, allowing relabelling of the incident half-edges and repeated occurrences of a generator. These
decorated graphs form a category indexing a homotopy-colimit resolution of the nerve.
Recall from Definition 2.24 that a decoration of a graph is a tuple assigning to each vertex an element of
a genus-graded sequence in the appropriate genus and valence. Let 𝒜𝐺 be the category whose objects
are graphs 𝑅 ∈ ℳ equipped with an  (𝑋𝐺 )-decoration 𝑝 such that each vertex decoration belongs
to the symmetric-group orbit of a generator of 𝑋𝐺 . We regard 𝑝 as an element of (N (𝑋𝐺 ))𝑅 . A
morphism (𝑅, 𝑝) → (𝑅′ , 𝑝′ ) is a graphical embedding 𝑗 ∶ 𝑅 → 𝑅′ in the sense of [32, Definitions 1.13
and 1.34] that preserves the decorations, meaning 𝑗 ∗ 𝑝′ = 𝑝. The exceptional edge carries the empty
tuple.
These decorated graphs determine a diagram
                           𝒜𝐺 ⟶ dMod(ℰ ),            (𝑅, 𝑝) ⟼ ℳ[𝑅] × {𝑝},
whose value at (𝐺, (𝑥1 , … , 𝑥𝑘 )) is 𝑍𝐺 . By Yoneda, the decorations give compatible maps ℳ[𝑅] ×
{𝑝} → N (𝑋𝐺 ). The following lemma shows that the induced map from the homotopy colimit of
this diagram is an entrywise weak equivalence.

Lemma B.4. The canonical map
                                        (          )
                                 hocolim ℳ[𝑅] × {𝑝} ⟶ N (𝑋𝐺 )
                                 (𝑅,𝑝)∈𝒜𝐺

is an entrywise weak equivalence in Fun(ℳ op , ℰ ).
                                      GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                        69


Proof. We first take ℰ = 𝒮 and consider the diagram (𝑅, 𝑝) ↦ ℳ[𝑅] × {𝑝} indexed by 𝒜𝐺 . By
Yoneda, each decoration determines a map ℳ[𝑅] × {𝑝} → N (𝑋𝐺 ), given at a graph 𝐻 by (𝑓 , 𝑝) ↦
𝑓 ∗ 𝑝. These maps are compatible with morphisms in 𝒜𝐺 : if 𝑗 ∶ (𝑅, 𝑝) → (𝑅′ , 𝑝′ ), then 𝑗 ∗ 𝑝′ = 𝑝, and
hence (𝑗𝑓 )∗ 𝑝′ = 𝑓 ∗ 𝑝.
Evaluating the simplicial replacement of this diagram at 𝐻 gives the simplicial set with 𝑛-simplices
                                    ∐
                                                 Homℳ (𝐻, 𝑅0 ) × {𝑝0 },
                              (𝑅0 ,𝑝0 )⟶⋯⟶(𝑅𝑛 ,𝑝𝑛 )

which represents the homotopy colimit evaluated at 𝐻. It is the nerve of the category of elements of
(𝑅, 𝑝) ↦ Homℳ (𝐻, 𝑅)×{𝑝}. Its objects are triples (𝑅, 𝑝, 𝑓 ), and a morphism (𝑅, 𝑝, 𝑓 ) → (𝑅′ , 𝑝′ , 𝑓 ′ )
is a morphism 𝑗 ∶ (𝑅, 𝑝) → (𝑅′ , 𝑝′ ) making the triangle

                                                      𝐻
                                             𝑓            𝑓′


                                        𝑅             𝑗
                                                               𝑅′

commute. The canonical map to (N (𝑋𝐺 ))𝐻 sends (𝑅, 𝑝, 𝑓 ) to 𝑓 ∗ 𝑝.
Fix 𝑦 ∈ (N (𝑋𝐺 ))𝐻 . We show that the full subcategory defined by 𝑓 ∗ 𝑝 = 𝑦 has an initial object. If
𝐻 =↕, this is (↕, ∅, id), since every graphical map from the exceptional edge is an embedding.
Suppose that 𝐻 has at least one vertex. For each 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉𝐻 , choose a decorated graph 𝑅𝑣 representing
𝑦𝑣 , with each vertex decoration belonging to the symmetric-group orbit of a generator of 𝑋𝐺 and
with its boundary identified with 𝑛𝑏𝐻 (𝑣). If 𝑦𝑣 is the unit, take 𝑅𝑣 to be the exceptional edge. Graph
substitution gives 𝑅𝑦 = 𝐻{𝑅𝑣 }𝑣∈𝑉𝐻 , its decoration 𝑝𝑦 , and a canonical active map 𝑓𝑦 ∶ 𝐻 → 𝑅𝑦
satisfying 𝑓𝑦∗ 𝑝𝑦 = 𝑦.
For another triple (𝑅, 𝑝, 𝑓 ) with 𝑓 ∗ 𝑝 = 𝑦, choose an embedding 𝑅′𝑣 → 𝑅 representing each vertex
image 𝑓1 (𝑣). The induced decoration represents 𝑦𝑣 , so Definition 2.24 gives a boundary-preserving
isomorphism 𝑅𝑣 ≅ 𝑅′𝑣 respecting the decorations. Graph substitution then gives an embedding 𝑗 ∶
𝑅𝑦 → 𝑅 with 𝑗𝑓𝑦 = 𝑓 and 𝑗 ∗ 𝑝 = 𝑝𝑦 ; see [32, Proposition 1.38 and Lemma 1.41].
To show uniqueness, let 𝑗1 , 𝑗2 ∶ 𝑅𝑦 → 𝑅 be two such embeddings. Uniqueness of active–embedding
factorisation [32, Proposition 2.14] gives an isomorphism 𝜃 ∶ 𝑅𝑦 → 𝑅𝑦 with 𝑗1 = 𝑗2 𝜃 and 𝜃𝑓𝑦 = 𝑓𝑦 .
Thus 𝜃 preserves 𝑝𝑦 and, since 𝑓𝑦 is active, fixes the boundary pointwise.
If 𝑅𝑦 is the exceptional edge, this gives 𝜃 = id. Otherwise, a fixed boundary leg determines a fixed
incident vertex. Its decoration lies in a free symmetric-group orbit, so preserving this decoration forces
𝜃 to fix every incident half-edge. Connectedness propagates this argument through the graph, giving
𝜃 = id. Hence 𝑗1 = 𝑗2 , and (𝑅𝑦 , 𝑝𝑦 , 𝑓𝑦 ) is initial.
Since morphisms of triples preserve 𝑓 ∗ 𝑝, the category of elements is a disjoint union of contractible
categories indexed by (N (𝑋𝐺 ))𝐻 . The canonical map in the statement is therefore an entrywise weak
equivalence.
For groupoids, apply the entrywise left derived functor of Π1 : it preserves homotopy colimits and
sends the discrete simplicial objects in the statement to the corresponding discrete groupoids. □

Lemma B.5. For 𝐺 ∈ ℳ, the objects 𝑍𝐺,∗ and N (𝑋𝐺 ) are weakly equivalent in 𝑆 dMod(ℰ )∗,𝑅 .

through the reduction, giving 𝑍𝐺,∗ → N (𝑋𝐺 ).

Entrywise Π1 sends the Segal-core localisation maps to their groupoid counterparts, so its left derived

Lemma B.6. If 𝑋 ∈ dMod(ℰ )∗,𝑝 is cofibrant, then the derived unit
                                                (      )
                                      𝑋 ⟶ N 𝜏(𝑋)𝑓
is a local equivalence in 𝑆 dMod(ℰ )∗,𝑝 .

Proof. For ℰ = 𝒮 , choose a simplicial resolution 𝑋∙ of 𝑋 by coproducts of reduced representables.
This is obtained from the free–forgetful resolution of reduced presheaves in each simplicial degree by
taking the diagonal; compare [21, Section 9.2 and Proposition 9.11]. The degreewise augmentations
split after evaluation at each graph, and the latching maps are inclusions of coproduct summands.
Thus 𝑋∙ is Reedy cofibrant in the projective model structure.
                                                 ∐
Here 𝑟 denotes the resolution degree, with 𝑋𝑟 = 𝑖∈𝐼𝑟 𝑍𝐺𝑟,𝑖 ,∗ . The augmentation induces an entrywise
weak equivalence |𝑋∙ | → 𝑋, where realisation takes the diagonal of the two simplicial directions. In

(B.6)                                  𝑋 ≃ |𝑋∙ | ≃ hocolim

Since 𝜏 is left Quillen, 𝜏𝑋∙ is Reedy cofibrant, with 𝜏𝑋𝑟 ≅  ( 𝑖∈𝐼𝑟 𝑋𝐺𝑟,𝑖 ). Moreover, 𝜏 acts de-
greewise and therefore commutes with realisation. Applying it to the augmentation between cofibrant
objects gives a weak equivalence |𝜏𝑋∙ | ≅ 𝜏|𝑋∙ | → 𝜏𝑋.
Since the entrywise diagonal preserves finite products, N|𝜏𝑋∙ | ≅ |N𝜏𝑋∙ |. The latter realisation com-
putes the homotopy colimit in the reduced projective model structure: a Reedy cofibrant replacement
of N𝜏𝑋∙ is an entrywise weak equivalence in each resolution degree, and taking diagonals preserves
these weak equivalences. As N also preserves entrywise weak equivalences, we obtain

By (B.5), the unit 𝑋𝑟 → N𝜏𝑋𝑟 is a local equivalence for every 𝑟. Taking homotopy colimits and using
naturality of the unit gives the commutative square
                                                     ∼
                                     ∣ 𝑋∙ ∣                      𝑋

                                                              (     )
                                   ∣ N𝜏𝑋∙ ∣      ∼           N 𝜏(𝑋)𝑓 .

The top map is the augmentation, the bottom map is induced by |𝜏𝑋∙ | → 𝜏𝑋 → 𝜏(𝑋)𝑓 , and the
vertical maps are induced by the unit. The left-hand map is a local equivalence, while the horizontal
maps are entrywise weak equivalences. Hence the right-hand map, which is the derived unit at 𝑋, is
a local equivalence.                                                                               □
                                                                                                ∼
Remark B.7. For ℰ = 𝒢 , let 𝑋 be projectively cofibrant and choose a cofibrant replacement 𝐴 ←←←→
                                                                                                ← B𝑋
in dMod(𝒮 )∗,𝑝 . Then Π1 𝐴 → 𝑋 is an entrywise equivalence between cofibrant objects.
As in the proof of Lemma B.5, entrywise Π1 is left Quillen after localisation. Preservation of finite
products and the corresponding adjunctions give Π1 N ≅ NΠ1 and Π1 𝜏 ≅ 𝜏Π1 . Applying Π1 to the
simplicial-set case therefore shows that Π1 𝐴 → N𝜏(Π1 𝐴) is a local equivalence; the simplicial fibrant
replacement does not affect this conclusion because Π1 preserves weak equivalences.
Naturality of the unit gives a commutative square
                                                         ∼
                                        Π1 𝐴                      𝑋


                                      N𝜏(Π1 𝐴)           ∼       N𝜏𝑋.
72                                L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


The left-hand map is a local equivalence by the simplicial-set case. The top map is an entrywise
equivalence, and so is the bottom map, since 𝜏 preserves weak equivalences between cofibrant objects
and N preserves entrywise weak equivalences. Hence the right-hand map is a local equivalence. Every
modular operad in groupoids is fibrant, so this is the required derived unit.

Proof of Theorem B.2. By Proposition B.3, 𝜏 ⊣ N is a Quillen adjunction for the projective model
structure. The nerve of a fibrant modular operad is projectively fibrant and satisfies the Segal condition
strictly. Hence the adjunction descends to a Quillen adjunction
                                                              𝜏
                                     𝑆 dMod(ℰ )∗,𝑝                     Mod(ℰ ).
                                                              N

                                                                                 ∼
Let  be fibrant and choose a cofibrant replacement 𝑞 ∶ 𝑄N() ←←←→
                                                                 ← N() in dMod(ℰ )∗,𝑝 . The derived
counit is represented by 𝑒 = 𝜖 ◦𝜏𝑞.
                                                   ∼
Choose a fibrant replacement 𝑗 ∶ 𝜏𝑄N() ←←←→
                                           ← (𝜏𝑄N())𝑓 that is a trivial cofibration. Since  is
fibrant, 𝑒 extends to 𝑒𝑓 ∶ (𝜏𝑄N())𝑓 → . The triangle identity gives
                                         N(𝑒𝑓 )◦N(𝑗)◦𝜂𝑄N() = 𝑞.
Lemma B.6 implies that N(𝑗)◦𝜂𝑄N() is a local equivalence. Since 𝑞 is also a local equivalence, so is
N(𝑒𝑓 ).
Both its source and target are local fibrant, so N(𝑒𝑓 ) is an entrywise weak equivalence; see [38, 3.2.13].
Evaluating at 𝐶(𝑔,𝑛+1) shows that 𝑒𝑓 is a weak equivalence in Mod(ℰ ), and hence so is 𝑒 = 𝑒𝑓 ◦𝑗. Thus
the derived counit is a weak equivalence, and the right derived functor of N is fully faithful.          □

           A PPENDIX C. T HE COMPLEX OF MARKINGS OVER PANTS DECOMPOSITIONS
In this appendix, we prove that the complex of markings (𝑆) introduced in Section 2.7 is connected
and simply connected. This is the complex used in the proof of Theorem 3.18.
The 2-dimensional CW complex (𝑆) is closely related to the complex 𝑚𝑎𝑥 (𝑆) of [4]. The main
difference is that (𝑆) only retains markings over pants decompositions: disc and cylinder compo-
nents are not allowed. Following the strategy of [4, 9], our proof proceeds by comparing (𝑆) with
a simply connected complex via a forgetful map and applying two general comparison results.
The first lemma allows us to deduce simple connectivity of the target from that of the source.

Lemma C.1. Let 𝐴 be a simply connected CW complex, and let 𝑓 ∶ 𝐴 → 𝐵 be a continuous map
such that every loop in 𝐵 lifts to a loop in 𝐴. Then 𝐵 is simply connected.           □

The following proposition gives a criterion for deducing simple connectivity of the source from that
of the target.

Proposition C.2. Let 𝐴 and 𝐵 be 2-dimensional CW complexes, and let 𝜋 ∶ 𝐴[1] → 𝐵 [1] be a
surjective map of their 1-skeleta. Suppose that
     (1) 𝐵 is connected and simply connected.
     (2) For every vertex 𝑏 ∈ 𝐵, the fiber 𝜋 −1 (𝑏) is connected, and every loop contained in 𝜋 −1 (𝑏) is
         contractible in 𝐴.
                 𝑒                                     𝑒′                  𝑒′′
     (3) Let 𝑏1 ⇝ 𝑏2 be an edge in 𝐵, and let 𝑎′1 ⇝ 𝑎′2 and 𝑎′′
                                                             1
                                                                ⇝ 𝑎′′
                                                                   2
                                                                      be two lifts to 𝐴. Then there exist
                     𝑒1                       𝑒2

                         in 𝜋 −1 (𝑏1 ) and 𝑎′2 ⇝ 𝑎′′

    (4) For every 2-cell 𝑋 in 𝐵, its boundary 𝜕𝑋 can be lifted to a contractible loop in 𝐴.
Then 𝐴 is connected and simply connected.                                                               □

The proofs of the preceding two results are standard. Proposition C.2 is established by a cellular lifting
argument: one projects a loop in 𝐴 to 𝐵 and lifts a nullhomotopy back to 𝐴 cell by cell. Conditions (2)
and (3) are used to compare different choices of lifts, and condition (4) is applied to lift the 2-cells of
𝐵. We omit the details.
We now recall the pants complex of Hatcher–Lochak–Schneps [35] in a form suited to our setting. Let
𝑆 = (𝑆, 𝛿) be a labelled surface of type (𝑔, 𝑛 + 1), with 𝑛 ≥ 0. Recall that a pants decomposition of
𝑆 is a cut system 𝐶 = {𝑐1 , … , 𝑐𝑘 } of pairwise disjoint simple loops on 𝑆 such that

                                                ⋃
                                                𝑘         ∐

                                                               𝑗
                                                𝑟=1
is a disjoint union of subsurfaces 𝑆𝑗 , each of which is of type (0, 3). The pants complex of 𝑆, denoted
(𝑆), is the 2-dimensional CW complex whose vertices are isotopy classes of pants decompositions on
𝑆, whose edges are generated by 𝐴-moves and 𝑆-moves between pants decompositions, and whose 2-
cells are attached along loops of type (3𝐴), (5𝐴), (3𝑆), (6𝐴𝑆), and (𝐶), in the sense of [35, Section 2].
By [35, Theorem 2], the complex (𝑆) is connected and simply connected.
Recall from Definition 2.37 that the complex of marked pants decompositions on 𝑆, denoted (𝑆),
has vertices given by marked pants decompositions (𝐶, [𝑚]) on 𝑆. The 1-cells are generated by 𝑇 -,
𝐴-, 𝐵-, and 𝑆-moves. The 2-cells are slight modifications of those appearing in [4]. Concretely, we

The complex of markings over the pair of pants 𝑃 , will be of particular relevance for us.

Lemma C.3. Let 𝑃 be a pair of pants. Then (𝑃 ) is connected and simply connected.

Proof. For a pair of pants 𝑃 , a vertex of (𝑃 ) is simply a marked pants decomposition of the form
(∅, [𝑚]), since the only pants decomposition of 𝑃 is the empty cut system.
Let max (𝑃 ) denote the complex of maximal markings on 𝑃 in the sense of [4, Section 5]. A vertex
of max (𝑃 ) is given by an isotopy class of a cut system
                                           𝐶 = {𝑐1 , … , 𝑐𝑘 }
together with a marking, such that cutting 𝑃 along 𝐶 decomposes it into pairs of pants, possibly
together with cylinder and disc components. In addition, one records a distinguished half-edge at
each vertex of the marking graph.
We compare max (𝑃 ) with (𝑃 ) by means of a map
                                      𝑞 ∶ max (𝑃 ) ⟶ (𝑃 ).
On vertices, the map 𝑞 forgets the cut system 𝐶 and the distinguished half-edges, and contracts those
edges of the marking graph that are not incident to a boundary component, thereby producing a vertex
(∅, [𝑚]) ∈ (𝑃 ).
On edges, the map 𝑞 behaves as follows. Moves of type 𝑇 and 𝐵 are either contracted to points or taken
to the corresponding edges of (𝑃 ). By contrast, moves of type 𝐹 forget cuts bounding cylindrical
components and therefore become trivial after passing to the empty cut system. Likewise, 𝐴-moves
                                      GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                      75




         F IGURE 17. Hexagon relations: Hexagon I (top left) and Hexagon II (bottom right)


are supported on a subsurface of type (0, 4), and also are contracted to points via 𝑞. Finally, a 𝑍-move
changes only the choice of distinguished half-edge, which is not retained in (𝑃 ). Thus 𝐴-, 𝐹 -, and
𝑍-edges are all sent to vertices.
This defines a cellular map on the 1-skeleton of max (𝑃 ). Moreover, each defining 2-cell of max (𝑃 )
maps either to the corresponding 2-cell of (𝑃 ) or to a degenerate loop. Hence 𝑞 extends to a cellular
76                              L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


map
                                       𝑞 ∶ max (𝑃 ) → (𝑃 ).

We next show that every loop in (𝑃 ) lifts to a loop in max (𝑃 ). Given a cellular loop in (𝑃 ),
each vertex in the loop is a marking of the form (∅, [𝑚]). Any such vertex can be lifted to one in
max (𝑃 ) by choosing a distinguished half-edge for the marking [𝑚]. For instance, we may choose
the half-edge incident to the boundary component labelled 0. In this way, all vertices in the loop in
(𝑃 ) lift to vertices in max (𝑃 ).
Since every edge of (𝑃 ) is a 𝑇 - or 𝐵-move, each 𝑒𝑖 lifts to an edge path in max (𝑃 ) consisting
of the corresponding 𝑇 - or 𝐵-move, together with 𝑍-moves, if necessary, to adjust the distinguished
half-edge before or after performing that move. Concatenating these lifted edge paths yields a loop in
max (𝑃 ) projecting to the original loop in (𝑃 ).
By [4, Theorem 4.9], the complex max (𝑃 ) is connected and simply connected. Lemma C.1 therefore
implies that (𝑃 ) is hence connected and simply connected as well.                            □

To study the complex of markings (𝑆) for more general surfaces, we compare (𝑆) with (𝑆) via
the natural forgetful map 𝐹 ∶ (𝑆) ⟶ (𝑆) defined on vertices by
                                            𝐹 (𝐶, [𝑚]) = 𝐶,
that is, by sending a marked pants decomposition to its underlying pants decomposition.
We use this to prove the main result of this section:

Proposition (Proposition 2.38). Let 𝑆 = (𝑆, 𝛿, {𝑥𝑖 }𝑛𝑖=0 ) be a parametrised, labelled surface of type
(𝑔, 𝑛 + 1), 𝑛 ≥ 0. Then (𝑆), the complex of markings over pants decompositions on 𝑆, is connected
and simply connected.

Proof. Let
                                         𝐹 ∶ (𝑆) ⟶ (𝑆)
be the forgetful map sending a marked pants decomposition (𝐶, [𝑚]) to its underlying pants decompo-
sition 𝐶. By [35, Theorem 2], the pants complex (𝑆) is connected and simply connected. We now
verify the hypotheses of Proposition C.2 for the map 𝐹 . By construction, 𝐹 is surjective on vertices
and edges.
Checking condition (2): Let 𝐶 be a vertex of (𝑆), that is, a pants decomposition of 𝑆. The fibre
𝐹 −1 (𝐶) is the subcomplex of (𝑆) whose vertices are markings with underlying pants decomposi-
tion 𝐶, and whose edges and cells are precisely those built from moves that leave 𝐶 unchanged. In
particular, its 1-cells are the 𝑇 - and 𝐵-moves, and its 2-cells are the relations among these moves
that do not alter the underlying pants decomposition, namely commutativity of disjoint moves, the
boundary twist relations, the twist–braiding relation, and the Gluing relations for Dehn twists.
We next show that 𝐹 −1 (𝐶) is connected and simply connected. Let 𝑆1 , … , 𝑆𝑘 be the pair-of-pants
components obtained as the closures of the connected components of 𝑆 ⧵ 𝐶. For each curve 𝑐 ∈ 𝐶,
choose a basepoint on 𝑐. These basepoints induce marked points on the corresponding boundary
components of the 𝑆𝑖 , so that each 𝑆𝑖 becomes a parametrised, labelled surface of type (0, 3) (Defini-
tion 2.26). In particular, a marking on each 𝑆𝑖 may be viewed as an embedded corolla meeting each
boundary component at the chosen marked point.
Using these basepoints, we may glue markings on the 𝑆𝑖 along the cut curves: if two boundary com-
ponents of 𝑆𝑖 and 𝑆𝑗 arise from the same curve 𝑐 ∈ 𝐶, then we identify them so that the marked points
determined by the chosen basepoint of 𝑐 agree. In this way we obtain a map
                                 (𝑆1 ) × ⋯ × (𝑆𝑘 ) ⟶ 𝐹 −1 (𝐶),
sending a tuple of markings on the pair-of-pants components to the induced marking on 𝑆 with under-
lying pants decomposition 𝐶. Since each (𝑆𝑖 ) is connected and simply connected by Lemma C.3,
                                      GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                     77


the product
(C.1)                                    (𝑆1 ) × ⋯ × (𝑆𝑘 )
is also connected and simply connected.
The gluing map is surjective on vertices: any marking on 𝑆 with underlying pants decomposition 𝐶
can be isotoped so that it intersects each curve 𝑐 ∈ 𝐶 at its chosen marked point. Such a marking then
restricts, after cutting along 𝐶, to markings on the pair-of-pants components 𝑆1 , … , 𝑆𝑘 , and gluing
these restrictions recovers the original marking. It is also surjective on 1-cells, since every edge of
𝐹 −1 (𝐶) is a 𝑇 - or 𝐵-move, hence is supported on a single pair-of-pants component, and therefore
is obtained by applying the corresponding edge in one factor (𝑆𝑖 ) while leaving the other factors
fixed.
The gluing map is not injective on vertices, because isotopies of the glued surface 𝑆 are not required
to preserve the chosen basepoints on the cut curves. In fact, the map (C.1) is the quotient of
                                         (𝑆1 ) × ⋯ × (𝑆𝑘 )
by an action of ℤ[𝐶], the free abelian group generated by the curves of the cut system 𝐶. For each
𝑐 ∈ 𝐶, the corresponding generator acts on a tuple
                                (𝑚1 , … , 𝑚𝑘 ) ∈ (𝑆1 ) × ⋯ × (𝑆𝑘 )
as follows. If 𝑐 bounds two distinct components 𝑆𝑖 and 𝑆𝑗 , then
                       𝑐 ⋅ (𝑚1 , … , 𝑚𝑘 ) = (𝑚1 , … , 𝐷𝑐 𝑚𝑖 , … , 𝐷𝑐−1 𝑚𝑗 , … , 𝑚𝑘 ).
If 𝑐 corresponds to two boundary components 𝑐1 , 𝑐2 of a single component 𝑆𝑖 , then
                           𝑐 ⋅ (𝑚1 , … , 𝑚𝑘 ) = (𝑚1 , … , 𝐷𝑐1 𝐷𝑐−1 𝑚𝑖 , … , 𝑚𝑘 ).
                                                                 2

It is simple to check that this is a well-defined action on the vertices of (𝑆1 ) × ⋯ × (𝑆𝑘 ) which
extends to a free and properly discontinuous action on the entire space. Then the quotient has funda-
mental group ℤ𝐶 generated by loops of the type
                                       (id𝑆𝑖 ∪𝑐 𝑇𝑐 ) (𝑇𝑐−1 ∪𝑐 id𝑆𝑗 ),
and similarly in the nonseparating case. Since these loops are precisely the boundaries of the 2-
cells (Gluing relations for Dehn twists) in 𝐹 −1 (𝐶), we conclude that 𝐹 −1 (𝐶) is connected and simply
connected.
It remains to verify conditions (3) and (4) of Proposition C.2.
Checking condition (3): this concerns comparing different lifts of edges in (𝑆). Recall that the edges
of (𝑆) are given by 𝐴- and 𝑆-moves. We begin with the former.
        𝑒
Let 𝐶 ⇝ 𝐶 ′ be an 𝐴-move in (𝑆) exchanging the curve 𝑎 ∈ 𝐶 with 𝑎′ ∈ 𝐶 ′ , and let 𝑒1 and 𝑒2 be two
lifts of 𝑒. Then 𝑒1 and 𝑒2 must be edge-paths of the form 𝛾 ′ ◦𝐴±1
                                                                𝑎,𝑎′
                                                                     ◦𝛾, where 𝛾 and 𝛾 ′ are edge-paths
in 𝐹 −1 (𝐶) and 𝐹 −1 (𝐶 ′ ), respectively. Since Condition (2) has already been verified, we may assume
that
                       𝑒1 ∶ (𝐶, 𝑚1 ) ⇝ (𝐶 ′ , 𝑚′1 ) and 𝑒2 ∶ (𝐶, 𝑚2 ) ⇝ (𝐶 ′ , 𝑚′2 )
are single edges corresponding to 𝐴-moves. By the 2-cell (Self-duality of associativity), we may
assume without loss of generality that these are 𝐴-moves, rather than their inverses.
For a fixed marking over the cut system 𝐶, there is a unique 𝐴-move exchanging 𝑎 ∈ 𝐶. We now
analyse how the markings 𝑚1 and 𝑚2 may differ.
By the definition of the 𝐴-move (Definition 2.37), there exist diffeomorphisms 𝜙1 , 𝜙2 ∶ 𝑆 ′ → 𝑆 such
that 𝜙1 (𝑎) = 𝜙2 (𝑎) = 𝑐, and both 𝜙1 ◦𝑚1 and 𝜙2 ◦𝑚2 define the standard marking on 𝑆 . Moreover,
we have 𝛼𝜙1 (𝑎′ ) = 𝛼𝜙2 (𝑎′ ) = 𝑐 ′ , and both 𝛼◦𝜙1 ◦𝑚′1 and 𝛼◦𝜙2 ◦𝑚′2 define the standard marking on
𝑆 . By Lemma C.4, we may further assume that 𝜙1 and 𝜙2 send the same boundary components of
𝑆 ′ to the components {1, 2} of 𝑆 .
78                               L. BASUALDO BONATTO AND M. ROBERTSON


It follows that 𝜙1 ◦𝜙−12
                          is a diffeomorphism of 𝑆 preserving each component of the standard pants
decomposition. In particular, it is isotopic to a diffeomorphism fixing the curve 𝑐 pointwise. Thus, we
may regard 𝜙1 ◦𝜙−1  2
                       as a pair of diffeomorphisms of the two pairs of pants in 𝑆 ′ , that fix the boundary
components of the pants corresponding to 𝑐. From the description of the mapping class group of a
pair of pants, it follows that 𝜙1 ◦𝜙−12
                                          is a composition of Dehn twists along 𝑐 and the other boundary
components, together with possible braiding morphisms that fix 𝑐.
On the other hand,
                                      𝛼𝜙1 (𝛼𝜙2 )−1 = 𝛼(𝜙1 𝜙−1
                                                           2
                                                              )𝛼 −1
is a diffeomorphism of 𝑆      fixing 𝑐 ′ . This implies that 𝜙1 ◦𝜙−1
                                                                  2
                                                                     also fixes 𝛼 −1 (𝑐 ′ ). In particular, by
considering intersection numbers, we see that 𝜙1 ◦𝜙−1
                                                   2
                                                      cannot involve Dehn twists about 𝑐, since such
twists would not preserve 𝛼 −1 (𝑐 ′ ).
It follows that 𝜙1 ◦𝜙−1
                     2
                        is either a composition of Dehn twists along boundary components, or such a
composition followed by the pair of braidings (one on each pair-of-pants component) represented on
the right-hand side of Figure 18. Hence we may assume that 𝑚1 and 𝑚2 differ from one of the two
markings in Figure 18 only by Dehn twists along the boundary components.




                              F IGURE 18. Two standard markings on 𝑆0,4

If 𝑚1 and 𝑚2 are obtained from the same marking in Figure 18 by Dehn twists along the boundary
components, then the required commutativity of the square follows from the 2-cell (Boundary twist
relations).
It remains to consider the case where 𝑚1 and 𝑚2 arise from different markings in Figure 18. Without
loss of generality, assume that 𝑚1 and 𝑚2 correspond to the left and right markings, respectively. Then
Figure 19 gives the required commutative square.
A very similar argument works for the lifts of the S-move, and can be found in [4, Section 7.7]. Here,
the contractible square is obtained using the (Commutativity of disjoint moves) and the genus-one
relation for (𝑔, 𝑛) = (1, 1) given by (G1.1).
Checking condition (4): now we must show that the boundary of each standard 2-cell of (𝑆) lifts to
a contractible loop in (𝑆). In fact, even more, we have that each such boundary can be lifted to the
boundary of a standard 2-cell in (𝑆).
The correspondence is as follows:
      ∙ The boundary of the 2-cell (3𝐴) can be lifted to the boundary of the 2-cell corresponding to
        the hexagon relations; see Figure 17;
      ∙ The boundary of the 2-cell (5𝐴) can be lifted to the boundary of the 2-cell corresponding to
        the pentagon relation; see Figure 16;
      ∙ The boundary of the 2-cell (3𝑆) can be lifted to the boundary of the 2-cell corresponding to
        the Genus-one relation (G1.2); see Figure 7b;
      ∙ The boundary of the 2-cell (6𝐴𝑆) can be lifted to the boundary of the 2-cell corresponding
        to the Genus-one relation for (𝑔, 𝑛) = (1, 2); see Figure 8;
      ∙ The boundary of the 2-cell (𝐶) can be lifted to the boundary of the 2-cell corresponding to
        the relation (Commutativity of disjoint moves).
                                             GALOIS ACTIONS ON SURFACES                                                   79




                         F IGURE 19. Commutative diagram checking condition (3)

Thus the boundary of each 2-cell of (𝑆) lifts to a loop bounding a 2-cell of (𝑆). Hence condi-
tion (4) holds. Proposition C.2 now implies that (𝑆) is connected and simply connected.      □

Lemma C.4. Let 𝜙 ∶ 𝑆 ′ → 𝑆                be a diffeomorphism witnessing an 𝐴-move, as in Definition 2.37,
and let {𝑐, 𝑐 ′ } denote the boundary components of 𝑆 ′ that are mapped to the boundary components
{1, 2} of 𝑆 . Then there exists a diffeomorphism 𝜙′ ∶ 𝑆 ′ → 𝑆                     witnessing the same 𝐴-move such
that the boundary components {𝑐, 𝑐 ′ } are mapped to {0, 3}.

                       𝐴𝑎,𝑏
Proof. Let (𝐶, 𝑚) ⇝ (𝐶 ′ , 𝑚′ ) be an 𝐴-move on (𝑆) and let 𝑚|𝑆 ′ and 𝑚′𝑆 ′ denote representatives
of the markings 𝑚 and 𝑚′ restricted to 𝑆 ′ that, together with 𝜙, witnesses the 𝐴-move.
Let 𝑧 ∶ 𝑆 → 𝑆 denote a difffeomorphism that fixes the standard marking and on the boundary
components gives the permutation (13)(20) (this can be constructed using the standard marking and
the diagrammatic representation of mapping classes).
Define the diffeomorphism
                                                𝜙′ ∶= 𝑧𝑆 ◦𝜙.
By construction, 𝜙′ ◦𝑚|𝑆 ′ is still the standard marking on 𝑆 , and 𝛼◦𝜙′ ◦𝑚′𝑆 ′ is still the standard mark-
ing on 𝑆 . Hence 𝜙′ also witnesses this 𝐴-move and, by construction, takes the boundary components
{𝑐, 𝑐 ′ } to the boundaries {0, 3} on 𝑆 .                                                                                 □
